 2

ABSTRACT
This project aimed to connect material design to multi-environment component performance by
reconceptualizing how to design for AM to exploit the buckling and contact of small-scale features.
The main hypothesis was that by developing an understanding of how to build optimization-based
design approaches around cutting edge modeling and simulation (ModSim) for nonlinear solid
mechanics, we would enable navigation of the enlarged design space of AM to design for multiple
environments simultaneously. We addressed challenges around optimization-based design with
highy nonlinear solid mechanics phenomena by focusing on (1) robust nonlinear equation solvers,
(2) design problem formulations that make use of quantities computed robustly and directly by
the solver, (3) smooth mappings from design parameters to a conformal mesh of the geometric
domain, and (4) an analytic path-dependent adjoint approach that leverages automatic differenti-
ation. Through several examples across different applications of optimization-based design, we
demonstrate that highly nonlinear phenomena can be incorporated into a gradient-based optimiza-
tion approach to computational design if care is taken with respect to design parameterizations,
robust nonlinear solvers, and problem formulations.

1. INTRODUCTION

1.1. Motivation and Project Description

Additive Manufacturing (AM) is expected to play a large role in the labs-wide goals of accelerating
innovation and leading in modern engineering. More specifically, AM is seen as a key enabling
technology for increasing the agility of nuclear deterrence and other national security applications
involving complex coupled environments. However, the impact of AM on these initiatives has
not been as wide-ranging as hoped because – despite its unique qualities – the focus has mostly
been on detailed qualification to force AM components into pre-existing performance envelopes.
This paradigm fundamentally precludes the novel possibilities afforded by the geometric and
material flexibility of AM. In particular, the engineering of small-scale features to undergo buckling
and contact can cause large geometric and symmetry changes which provide responsiveness to
different environments. Despite almost a decade of observing such behavior, there exists no way
to systematically design for AM to exploit it. Our goal for this project was to connect material
design to multi-environment component performance by reconceptualizing how to design for AM
to exploit the buckling and contact of small-scale features. The main hypothesis was that by
developing an understanding of how to build optimization-based design approaches around cutting
edge modeling and simulation (ModSim) for nonlinear solid mechanics, we would enable navigation
of the enlarged design space of AM to design for multiple environments simultaneously.

Such an understanding can have important and immediate impacts on the nuclear deterrence mission
by helping to inform the design of direct ink write (DIW) silicone compression pads. These pads
are some of the first AM components seeing widespread use in the current life-extension programs
and future systems due to their widely tunable mechanical response and superior ability to produce
complex geometries compared to traditional cellular silicone pads. Preload of these compression
pads induces widespread buckling and self contact [86] that significantly change the nonlinear
response if parameters such as filament spacing or diameter are varied. Reduced-order models can
be constructed in an attempt to explore this large space of possible responses [269, 222], but even
these are limited to a relatively small set of design variables. As such, the use of compression
pads is largely driven by pre-AM design considerations based on intuitive understandings linking
performance to design variables in simple motifs for pad structure. Moreover, these “preload
only" approaches focus on compression response and are not extendable to the many systems
requiring compression pads perform vibration and impulse mitigation for specific frequency inputs
and amplitudes. As a result, designers rely on costly and time-consuming Edisonian build and test
cycles for making incremental changes to established designs under environments representative of
use case scenarios. In contrast, the optimization-based design capabilities studied and developed in
this work were pursued with the goal of enabling rapid exploration of novel, optimized compression
pad designs informed by simulations of these environments under preload.

 19

1.2. Optimization-Based Design of Soft Metamaterials

Broadly speaking, the characteristic features of DIW compression pads – a wide range of nonlinear
responses driven by widespread buckling and self contact of small-scale features – puts them in
line with a class of materials known as “soft metamaterials". These materials are characterized
by their ability harness geometric instabilities to undergo large and controllable configurational
changes [195]. As such, they offer the promise of programmable responses to external stimuli,
making them desirable for applications such as soft robotics [218, 111, 59], shock and vibration
mitigation [264, 228, 64, 100, 237], and flexible electronics [141, 276].
In line with the metamaterial concept, the response of soft metamaterials is governed to a large
extent by the geometric and topological makeup of their small-scale features. However, a re-
liance upon highly nonlinear responses triggered by geometric instabilities makes the link between
structure and response in soft metamaterials far less intuitive than in their linear counterparts.
Computational design tools can aid in the advancement of soft metamaterials by providing a rig-
orous approach to navigate their complex and non-intuitive design space. The most mature of
these tools are optimization-based design approaches which obtain a design by formulating and
solving a PDE-constrained minimization problem. Shape and topology optimization approaches
fall in this category as they use gradient-based optimization to navigate a large design space defined
by different geometric parameterizations. Shape optimization approaches are focused on freely
evolving the boundary of a design [118, 250]. This can be done by directly evolving the coordinates
of nodes in a finite element mesh (parameter-free approaches) [201, 164] or by evolving geomet-
ric parameters linked to CAD (parametric approaches) [43]. Isogeometric analysis (IGA) [130]
offers a different avenue for shape optimization as it provides access to CAD parameters (NURBS
control points) while unifying the design and analysis models [255, 60, 215]. Compared to shape
optimization, topology optimization offers a more flexible geometric parameterization by directly
evolving the distribution of material within a given domain [33], thus allowing for new boundaries
to emerge. This is achieved by varying a continuous density-like field (density-based approaches)
or the iso-contours of an implicit level-set function (level-set approaches), among others [232].
The development of optimization-based design approaches for soft metamaterials is challenging due
to the need to account for complex nonlinear physics involving geometric instabilities and possible
inelastic material response. Nonlinear equation solvers that can handle such physics often have to
be tuned to specific systems in order to converge to desired tolerances. This becomes problematic
when such solvers are employed within optimization-based design, as a forward problem must be
solved over a new geometric domain for every iteration in the optimization process, requiring the
solver to work without manual tuning for each problem. Moreover, in order to compute gradients
smooth mappings are required between the design parameterization used to generate the geometric
domain and the response computed by the nonlinear solver. Formulating and differentiating such
smooth mappings becomes an error-prone and tedious process, especially when accounting for
path-dependent material behavior such as viscoelasticity.
In this report, we address the aforementioned challenges by focusing on (1) robust nonlinear equa-
tion solvers, (2) design problem formulations that make use of quantities computed robustly and
directly by the solver, (3) smooth mappings from design parameters to a conformal mesh of the
geometric domain, and (4) an analytic path-dependent adjoint approach that leverages automatic

 20

differentiation. Through several examples across different applications of optimization-based de-
sign, we demonstrate that highly nonlinear phenomena can be incorporated into a gradient-based
optimization approach to computational design if care is taken with respect to design parameteri-
zations, robust nonlinear solvers, and problem formulations.
Before detailing our approach, we first review related works focused on computational design ap-
proaches that can exploit the geometric instabilities occurring in structures and materials undergoing
large deformations.

1.3. Related Work

Several works exist that focus on shape optimization approaches aimed at tailoring the large
deformation elastic response of structures and materials. In [83, 61], curve parameterizations
were used to perform shape optimization of nonlinear elastic structures, but instabilities were not
addressed. A parameter-free shape optimization approach was used to maximize stiffness of thin-
walled structures undergoing geometric nonlinearities in [97], with instabilities indirectly addressed
by performing eigenvalue analyses to ensure positive-definiteness of the tangent stiffness of new
designs before evaluating the objective function. An IGA-based shape optimization approach for
design of structures composed of three-dimensional beams undergoing large deformations was
developed in [268, 267], showing the ability to reach a desired target deformed configuration or
response curve. This approach has been applied to design both finite metamaterial structures
and unit cells, including optimizing to avoid instability. IGA-based shape optimization has also
been employed to design auxetic behavior of metamaterial unit cells under large deformations
[266, 208]. Metamaterial unit cells were designed for various nonlinear behaviors — including
tailoring local buckling instabilities —in [275] by parameterizing pore shapes for optimization. In
[187] a parameter-free shape optimization approach was used to tailor the stress-strain response of
finite metamaterial samples undergoing large deformations, including buckling instabilities.
There have also been several topology optimization approaches developed specifically to exploit
geometric instabilities within the context of large deformation elasticity. In the context of buckling
instabilities, most formulations focus on avoiding the onset of instability by increasing or constrain-
ing the load at which the critical point occurs, see e.g. [150, 175, 70, 284, 69]. Formulations
for harnessing snap-through instabilities have also been developed for a number of applications,
beginning with the pioneering studies [45, 46]. In [139] density-based topology optimization was
employed to induce bi-stability through snap-through with application to cardiovascular stents.
A density-based approach to maximize the elastic energy absorption resulting from snap-through
in a metamaterial-inspired geometry was presented in [57]. Similarly, design of metamaterials
exhibiting snap-through was considered using a density-based approach in [76] and extended in
[75, 77]. In [171] a multi-material density-based topology optimization approach was employed
to tailor the stress-strain response of structures undergoing large deformations, including the those
exhibiting snap-through. In [256] a density-based topology optimization approach was developed
to exploit snap-through for bi-stability with application to mechanical logic gates.
In addition to optimization-based design approaches, there has been an increased interest lately
in the application of machine learning techniques to carry out computational design. As with

 21

shape and topology optimization the majority of studies have focused on linear properties, but a
few notable works have emerged with the aim of tailoring nonlinear response. An evolutionary
algorithm employing a neural network was used to produce targeted nonlinear stress-strain response
of a unit cell parameterized by hinged quadrilaterals in [74], including target responses driven by
buckling instabilities. In [28] a denoising diffusion model was used to tailor the nonlinear stress-
strain response of a metamaterial unit cell, demonstrating the ability to program responses that
involve the geometry undergoing buckling instabilities and self-contact. In [116], target stress-
strain curves which could include instabilities were generated using a machine learning framework
consisting of neural networks trained on experimental data for forward evaluation combined with
generative neural networks to produce unit cell design designs for a given curve.

1.4. Approach and Outline

A distinguishing feature of our approach is the nonlinear equation solver we employ to handle
forward problems in a robust manner. We must be able to follow energetically-favorable equilibrium
paths through critical points to capture the response of structures undergoing geometric instabilities.
We must also solve for the quasi-static evolution of these structures through time to properly model
history-dependent material behavior such as viscoelasticity. To do both simultaneously, we cast
the search for stable mechanical equilibrium as a minimization problem [248], rather than merely
searching for roots in the residual force. This minimization viewpoint enables the solver to
distinguish between stable equilibrium configurations — which the structure can actually inhabit
— and unstable equilibrium configurations, which are spurious. The minimization approach
also avoids other problems seen with classic root-finding solvers, such as stagnation in the residual
magnitude at local minima that are not equilibrium configurations. We illustrate an implementation
of the minimization concept with a trust region method, and show that it confers the robustness
needed to solve for the quasi-static time histories of soft metamaterials without manual intervention
or tuning of algorithmic parameters. As such, our optimization-based design approach utilizes
optimization algorithms at two levels: (1) as a robust approach for finding stable mechanical
equilibrium in the presence of instabilities and viscoelastic relaxation, and (2) as a way of tailoring
the design of a structure to achieve certain design goals.

With this second level in mind, we focus on problem formulations and design parameterizations
that provide smooth mappings for gradient-based optimization. We consider a number of design
objectives that are enabled by the unique ability of our nonlinear solver to robustly and directly
compute certain quantities of interest. New geometric domains are obtained by mapping design
parameters to a conformal mesh in a smooth manner that can accommodate large shape changes
while avoiding pitfalls of fixed mesh approaches. To compute gradients, we modify an analytic
path-dependent adjoint approach to leverage automatic differentiation.

In Chapter 2 we discuss the need for robust and differentiable physics simulations and detail the
minimization approach for finding stable mechanical equilibrium in forward problems. This in-
cludes motivating and detailing the incremental energy minimization framework and specifying
the form of the viscoelastic model, trust region solver, and contact algorithm. In Chapter 3, we
introduce examples that demonstrate the unique ability of our solution approach to robustly and

 22

directly compute relevant quantities of interest in soft metamaterials. In Chapter 4 we introduce
the optimization-based design framework used throughout this work. We also present the different
quantities of interest used to formulate optimization problems and details on the adjoint sensitivity
analysis. Chapters 5 and 6 then detail two different optimization-based design approaches to tailor
the geometry of soft metamaterials. In Chapter 5, an approach for CAD-parameterized shape opti-
mization is laid out and applied to design soft metamaterials that exploit both geometric instabilities
and viscoelastic relaxation. Chapter 6 then details an approach to level set topology optimization
that provides conforming meshes of the geometry at each optimization iteration. We show how
this approach can be used for generative design of structures undergoing large deformations and
buckling. We then consider a different type of optimization-based design problem in Chapter 7:
the design of spatially-varying material layouts enabled by grayscale digital light processing. We
fabricate the optimized designs and show how the resulting distributions of soft and stiff phases
throughout a metamaterial structure drive a wide range buckling and self-contact responses that
are realized experimentally. Finally, Chapter 8 presents the development of a thermodynamically
consistent, gradient-enhanced continuum finite element framework for modeling instabilities, phase
transitions, and auxetic responses in architected materials and metamaterials. By capturing such
phenomena at the continuum scale, this model provides a way to extend the optimization-based
design approaches developed in preceding chapters beyond relatively small material samples to
component-scale geometries. Overall conclusions and project legacy are discussed in Chapter 9,
and some recommendations for follow-on work are provided.

 23

 24

2. ROBUST AND DIFFERENTIABLE PHYSICS SIMULATIONS

In order to be employed for optimization-based design, the forward simulations used to capture
quantities of interest must have two properties: they must be robust and they must be differen-
tiable. When considering forward simulations of complex nonlinear physics involving geometric
instabilities and possible inelastic material response, these properties aren’t trivial to obtain. For
one, existing nonlinear equation solvers typically require tuning of settings to specific problems in
order to meet desired tolerances. The need for such manual tuning is disqualifying for use with
optimization-based design where a new geometric domain is generated at each iteration. Second,
existing nonlinear equation solvers typically are built specifically to address the forward problem of
computing a quantity or quantities of interest for a given problem geometry and inputs. Outfitting
these solvers to also compute derivatives of these quantities of interest is a highly non-trivial task.
With that said, the properties of robustness and differentiability are becoming increasingly impor-
tant as physics simulations constitute key components for enabling complex digital workflows for
design, engineering, computer graphics, and robotics.

One application where robustness is a property of upmost importance for physics simulations is
computer graphics. As such, this community has pioneered a number of techniques for increasing
robustness of different physics simulations, including for nonlinear solid mechanics. Two major
sources of fragility in large deformation solid mechanics are (1) a loss of positive-definiteness of the
tangent stiffness near critical points corresponding to geometric instabilities, and (2) local inversion
of elements when large time steps result in large configuration changes. Early work addressing
the latter was presented in [133] where a formulation for large deformation solid mechanics that is
tolerant to element inversions was developed. This approach was extended in [244] to smoothly
provide forces beyond a threshold at which elements would typically invert, essentially eliminating
restrictions on time steps caused by element inversion. A method to ensure positive-definiteness
of the global tangent stiffness matrix and thus enable use of fast Conjugate Gradient solvers was
also presented in [244]. The resulting framework enabled fast finite element simulations capable
of handling extremely large deformations and element distortions. The inversion-tolerant approach
was further refined in [241] by proposing an invertible extension of elastic strain energy to replace
the earlier extensions of stresses. An alternative to the extension approach was presented in [236]
and [169] by employing a line search routine that checks each quadrature point for the largest step
size that would cause inversion and limits the maximum global step size to the minimum of these.
These pioneering approaches to robust nonlinear solid mechanics simulations have become standard
in the computer graphics community but are only recently making their way to the engineering
community [210, 211]. More generally, the idea of robust physics simulations is becoming more
mainstream in the engineering community as simulations are asked to play a larger role in digital
engineering workflows.

For these same reasons, differentiability is also becoming an important property for simulations.

 25

This is being driven in large part by the advance of artificial intelligence and machine learning
(AI/ML) workflows that aim to use physics solvers to enhance learning of physical phenomena
[225, 126]. Embedding physics simulations into training workflows instead of trying to infer
physics directly from training data is seen as an important step to translating AI/ML models to
real-world applications as it provides higher sample efficiency and improved adherence to physical
laws in the resulting models [27, 124, 72]. Differentiable finite element simulations are also
finding widespread use in robotics, where they are included in simulation-based training workflows
[128, 231]. In these applications, gradients of the physics simulations are used to enhance planning
and control. Recent advances in image reconstruction applications in computer graphics are driven
by the inclusion of differentiable physics simulations as well [203, 173]. While differentiable
physics simulations have long been developed and advocated for in the optimization-based design
community, the recent explosion of interest driven by the AI/ML community is driving rapid
advancement of new numerical tools and packages.
In what follows we lay out our approach to enabling robust and differentiable simulations of
nonlinear solid mechanics.

2.1. Robust Strategy for Finding Stable Mechanical Equilibrium

We are interested in the quasi-static motion of a body specified in a reference configuration Ω ⊂ R𝑑 .
At every time 𝑡 𝑘 in a sequence of discrete time points 𝑡 0 , 𝑡 1 , . . . , 𝑡 𝑛 , we aim to find the displacement
field 𝒖 𝑘 of each material point 𝑿 ∈ Ω. The given data are the body forces 𝒃¯ 𝑘 , the tractions 𝒕¯𝑘
applied on Γ𝑁 ⊂ 𝜕Ω, and the Dirichlet boundary conditions for the displacement 𝒖¯ 𝑘 applied on
Γ𝐷 ⊂ 𝜕Ω. Starting from the initial condition 𝒖 0 , the displacement at successive time points must
be found. This section details the minimization approach we employ for finding stable equilibrium
configurations 𝒖 𝑘 .

2.1.1. Motivation

As a motivating example, consider the force-displacement response of a structure exhibiting pro-
totypical instabilities shown in Figure 2-1. If this structure is driven under load (traction) control
from the origin, the structure will make a sudden jump in configuration from point 𝐶 to point 𝐸.
Similarly, under displacement control, the structure will jump from the configuration at point 𝐺
to point 𝐼. Such jumps correspond to non-equilibrium transitions associated with snap-through
and snap-back instabilities, respectively. Soft metamaterials that exploit these non-equilibrium
transitions have been the subject of intense study in recent years, owing to their ability to dissipate
or store energy in a recoverable manner, which can be useful for applications such as impact and
vibration mitigation [220, 64, 228], or actuation for soft robotics [111, 59]. Shaded areas in Figure
2-1 illustrate the energy that would be dissipated if these non-equilibrium transitions were traversed
in a single cycle. As we are interested in using optimization-based design to manipulate how such
soft metamaterials store or dissipate energy, we need a solution approach that can robustly and
directly compute these energetic quantities. To do so, the solution approach must be capable of
handling the jumps in equilibrium configuration between points 𝐶𝐸, 𝐷𝐵, 𝐺 𝐼, and 𝐻𝐹. However,

 26

 C load control Snap-back under
 E displacement control

 H

  A
 Displacement
 Figure 2-1. Illustration of force-displacement response for a structure undergoing snap-through
and snap-back instabilities (reproduced from Crisfield [65]). Shaded areas illustrate the energy that
 would be dissipated through non-equilibrium transitions in a single cycle.

making this jump in configuration presents numerous difficulties for nonlinear equation solvers
based on a root-finding algorithms.

A root search typically uses the last known equilibrium point as the initial guess. When dealing
with large jumps in configuration, the solver will be looking for a root very far away from the initial
guess, and is likely to fail. Even if a globalization method is employed in the root search, there can
still be problems. For example, a common globalization strategy is to use a line search, with a merit
function 𝜇(𝒖) = 𝑹(𝒖)𝑇 𝑹(𝒖), where 𝑹 is the nodal residual force function. However, there can be
configurations that lie in a neighborhood where the residual norm increases at any other point in
that neighborhood. For example, imagine that the prototype structure depicted in Figure 2-1 is in
an equilibrium configuration just to the left of point 𝐶, and that for the next time step, the external
force is increased to a value greater then that of 𝐶. At any configuration between points 𝐶 and 𝐷,
the residual norm will be larger than it is at the initial point just to the left of 𝐶. Since line search
algorithms usually reduce the initial step length, the solver will be trapped if the initial step length
does not take it past point 𝐷. No amount of cutbacks from the initial step will satisfy a sufficient
decrease condition in the merit function.

One alternative is to appeal to dynamics. In this case, there is no jump in configuration, because
the non-equilibrium transition is explicitly modeled. However, in our design problems — which we
believe to be typical — the time scale of the dynamic process is orders of magnitude smaller than
those of the loading and the viscous relaxation (see also the discussion in [96]). Computing the
dynamics would be computationally expensive. In other words, we have an effectively quasi-static
process and we do not want to use dynamics as regularization, we want a robust quasi-static solution

 27

algorithm.
Another strategy is to use continuation (i.e. “arc-length” or “path-following” methods), such as the
well-known methods of Riks [221] or Crisfield [65], to follow the unstable branch of the response
(𝐶𝐷𝐸 or 𝐺𝐻𝐼 in Figure 2-1). While this is undoubtedly useful for characterizing the equilibrium
response of such a structure, it cannot be used to quantify the dissipated energy directly, as the path
that includes the jumps in configuration is not being followed. As a result, complicated numerical
strategies for identifying critical points must be employed [76, 57]. Another drawback in the present
context is that these approaches do not follow the time history of the structure. Without the time
history, one cannot model inherently rate-dependent effects, such as viscoelasticity.
Finally, the quasi-Newton or projected-Newton approaches advocated for by the computer graphics
community [244] and making their way into the engineering community [210, 211] present another
alternative. These approaches use a root-finding approach but enable very large initial step lengths
by ensuring that the tangent stiffness matrix is always positive definite.

2.1.2. Minimization Approach

As an alternative to root-finding approaches, we instead pose the problem of finding the quasi-static
equilibrium path of a solid body as a sequence of minimization problems

is a necessary condition for the minimum principle (2.1) to be satisfied.

In this sense, the minimization approach and the virtual work approach are similar. However, the
minimum principle has a well known sufficient condition, which is that the second variation be
coercive. It is this additional information that differentiates the minimization approach from a
root-finding approach and allows us to construct more robust solvers.

Discretizing Eq. 2.5 with a Galerkin finite element method leads to the system of nonlinear
equations

                                             𝑹(𝒖) = 0,                                          (2.6)
where 𝒖 is the set of unconstrained nodal displacements, and 𝑹 is the nodal residual force function
which can be given by the derivative

 𝜕 𝐴(𝒖)
 𝑹(𝒖) =
 𝜕𝒖
Here we have slightly abused notation by using the same symbol 𝐴 for the discretized version of the
energy that operates on the nodal displacement vector as we have for the version that operates on the
corresponding field. While the condition that the residual vanishes, Eq. 2.6, remains unchanged
from a root-finding approach, the sufficient condition becomes

                                             𝑩(𝒖) ≻ 0,                                          (2.7)
where

 𝜕 𝑹(𝒖)
                                          𝑩(𝒖) =                                               (2.8)
 𝜕𝒖
is the tangent stiffness matrix. Now we have an excellent merit function: the energy functional
itself. As long as we ensure sufficient decreases in the energy, optimization theory provides
guarantees that we will find a minimizer. In this way we avoid the problem of failing to converge to
a solution. Moreover, this helps us avoid converging to unstable equilibrium configurations because
such configurations will be saddle points in the energy landscape. Whether or not the residual is
zero, we can continually scan for directions in which the tangent stiffness matrix is not positive
definite and be assured that these are directions that can decrease the energy, taking us away from
saddle points.

To extend the minimization approach to dissipative materials, we apply the theory of variational
constitutive updates. We give only a brief overview here, and refer to the literature for details
[205, 217]. Now, in addition to the current position field, the solid is endowed with internal
variables 𝒁 that describe the irreversible behavior of the material. We need to prescribe evolution
equations for 𝒁, but while doing so, we must ensure that Eq. (2.3) remains valid. That is, we require
that there remains a potential structure for the Piola stress. One way to ensure this is to write the
evolution equations in the form

 29

                       𝒁 𝑘+1 = arg min 𝜓(𝑭 𝑘+1 , 𝒁) − 𝜓(𝑭 𝑘 , 𝒁 𝑘 ) + Δ𝑡Φ 𝒁ˆ ,                    (2.9)
 𝒁

where 𝜓(𝑭, 𝒁) is the free energy density function (now a function of the deformation gradient and
the internal variables), 𝒁ˆ is a consistent numerical approximation to the time derivative 𝜕 𝒁/𝜕𝑡 ,
and Φ is the dissipation potential. The potential density 𝜙 to be used in Eq. 2.2 is then

It is important to note that since the internal variables 𝒁 are updated at each time step, the energy
functional changes at each time step and is hence able to describe irreversible behavior.
The variational constitutive formalism does not apply universally. However, it does cover a signif-
icant range of classes of material models. Virtually all associative plasticity models fit this form
[205]. Variational models have also been formulated for many other physics, including Cam-Clay
soil mechanics [204], piezoelectrics [192], and conveniently for our applications, viscoelasticity
[90]. Most importantly, it allows us to write the equilibrium problem with the minimization
structure, and therefore to use general purpose optimization algorithms to execute our simulations
robustly and efficiently.

2.1.3. Incremental Viscoelastic Potential

The specific form of potential density 𝜙 used to capture finite deformation viscoelastic effects in this
report is based on a multiplicative decomposition of the deformation gradient and follows the theory
presented originally in [219] and extended to variational form in [90]. The deformation gradient
𝑭 is decomposed into elastic 𝑭 𝑒 and inelastic (viscoelastic) 𝑭 𝑣 components, i.e. 𝑭 = 𝑭 𝑒 𝑭 𝑣 . A
generalized Maxwell model is used to account for viscoelasticity such that the free energy 𝜓 is split
as

                          𝜓 (𝑭, 𝒁) = 𝜓 (𝑭, 𝑭 𝑣 ) = 𝜓 𝑒𝑞 (𝑭) + 𝜓 𝑛𝑒𝑞 (𝑭, 𝑭 𝑣 )                    (2.11)
where the inelastic deformation gradient 𝑭 𝑣 plays the role of internal variable 𝒁. The equilibrium
free energy 𝜓 𝑒𝑞 accounts for elastic deformation as the time 𝑡 → ∞ and the non-equilibrium free
energy 𝜓 𝑛𝑒𝑞 accounts for elastic deformation in the Maxwell element. The viscous part of the
Maxwell element is accounted for in the dissipation potential Φ. In general, multiple Maxwell
branches can be included in the model, with each contributing a separate non-equilibrium term
𝜓 𝑛𝑒𝑞 and dissipation term Φ.
It remains to specify the form of the equilibrium 𝜓 𝑒𝑞 and non-equilibrium 𝜓 𝑛𝑒𝑞 free energy and
dissipation potential Φ. The equilibrium portion of the free energy is chosen to have the Neo-
Hookean form

2.1.4. Trust Region Solver

To solve the minimization problem, Eq. 2.1, standard unconstrained optimization algorithms such
as line-search or trust region can be used. In this work, we employ a variant of the precondi-
tioned Steihaug-Toint truncated conjugate-gradient method [240, 246, 248], which is a trust region
algorithm that naturally allows for inexact linear model problem solves. This approach is fairly
standard in the optimization community [202] and is known to have good convergence properties.
We have utilized the implementation of this algorithm in the Python-based finite element library
titled OptimiSM [249] which is built on the powerful automatic differentiation package JAX [42],
developed at Google primarily for machine-learning applications. With this, we are only required
to implement the energy functions, e.g. Eq. 2.18. The corresponding gradients and Hessian-vector
products are efficiently computed and just-in-time (jit) compiled for us by JAX. This code base
remains available for future prototyping of new ideas in computational mechanics [249].
For completeness we include the algorithm below. While this algorithm has been effective for the
problems we are interested in, alternative optimization algorithms which perform better for certain
problems are also possible.
Consider the unconstrained minimization problem

                                               min 𝑓 (𝒙).                                         (2.19)
 𝒙
which represents the equilibrium minimization problem in Eq. 2.1. This general problem can be
minimized by approximately solving a sequence of model problems, indexed by 𝑘, that are simpler
than the original problem. These model problems are also constrained such that the 𝑙2 -norm of the
increment in the solution must be within the current trust region radius

 𝒙 𝑘+1 − 𝒙 𝑘 = arg min 𝑚(𝒛; 𝒙 𝑘 )

of trust region iterations, minimum trust region size Δ𝑚𝑖𝑛 , and tolerance 𝜀 are adjusted based on
the specific problem.
The preconditioned Steihaug-Toint conjugate gradient method [240, 246] is used to minimize the
quadratic model problem 𝑚(𝒛), as outlined in Algorithm 2. The algorithm uses standard conjugate
gradient (CG) iterations in an attempt to minimize the model problem. However, two things
can differ from the standard CG algorithm: the model problem can be non-convex (an indefinite
Hessian), or the minimum solution can be outside the current trust region. To address this, if a
step in the CG algorithm finds a direction of negative curvature we return a 𝒛 which moves in that
direction, projected to the trust region boundary. In this case, the model problem is indicating that
moving in that direction as far as possible would significantly reduce 𝑓 (𝒙). Similarly, whenever a
CG step is taken outside of the trust region, we project that step back to the boundary and return
the projected step as the approximate solution. A symmetric positive definite (SPD) preconditioner
𝑴𝑘 ≻ 0 which is ideally an approximation to 𝑩−1  𝑘 = 𝑓,𝒙𝒙 (𝒙 𝑘 )
 −1 is used. The strategy for updating

these preconditioners is described below.
Automatic differentiation is used to compute the Hessian-vector products 𝑩 𝑘 𝒑, but if an approxima-
tion is used instead, the Polak–Ribiére formula for 𝛽 can be used to potentially improve robustness
[209]. The tolerance, 𝜀 𝑐𝑔 , for the conjugate gradient model problem solve is set to allow for inexact
solves in cases where the nonlinear residual is still large, but small enough when needed to ensure
convergence of the outer trust region 1. In particular, we use

  
                                  𝜀 𝑐𝑔 = max 0.2𝜀, 1 × 10−5 ∥ 𝒈 𝑘 ∥                             (2.23)

In practice, a quality preconditioner 𝑴𝑘 is essential for maximizing solver performance. However,

the cost to reassemble and refactor a preconditioner at every step of the algorithm can be significant,
so the preconditioner is only updated when the model problem solver, Algorithm 2 takes a large
number of CG iterations iterations, typically in the range 10-100. If this limit is reached, the
algorithm exits early, and either accepts or rejects the step as usual, and then immediately updates
the preconditioner for the next model problem solve. If the model problem solver requires less
than this iteration limit, the preconditioner is not updated. This ensures we are getting a quality
preconditioner when needed, but avoids updating the preconditioner when the problem is sufficiently
linear locally that a previously computed preconditioner is still accurate. We also update the
preconditioner when the cumulative count of iterations across model problems solves since the last
preconditioner update exceeds some amount (typically 50-500). These parameters can influence
the efficiency of the overall algorithm, but we have not found them to have a significant influence
on the robustness.

The preconditioner should be strictly positive definite for the linear conjugate gradient method
to function robustly. Here we choose to store the system sparse stiffness matrix and utilize the
CHOLMOD Cholesky decomposition from the SuiteSparse library [58]. The strategy used here
for ensuring the preconditioner is positive definite is fairly simple and standard [109]. We first try
to compute a Cholesky decomposition on the current Hessian 𝑓,𝒙𝒙 (𝒙 𝑘 ). If that succeeds, we know
the resulting preconditioner is positive definite. If the decomposition fails, we scale the diagonal
entries of the matrix by a factor 1 + 10 𝑘−1 𝜀, where 𝜀 = 1 × 10−7 , and 𝑘 is the count of how many
times the Cholesky decomposition has previously failed for this step. Provided that the initial
diagonal entries in the Hessian matrix are positive, this method will eventually yield a positive

 34

definite matrix which can be factorized for use as a preconditioner.

2.2. Barrier-Inspired Approach to Smooth Contact

2.2.1. Contact Kinematics

Let Ω 𝐴 and Ω𝐵 be two bodies which may be in contact. The boundary of Ω 𝐴 can be divided
into three distinct parts: Γ𝐷𝐴 where Dirichlet boundary conditions (displacements) are applied, Γ𝑁𝐴
where Neumann boundary conditions (tractions) are applied, and Γ𝐶𝐴 where contact with Ω𝐵 may
occur. The same applies to the boundary of Ω𝐵 . The surface Γ𝐴𝐵 is used to denote the surface that
arises when the bodies come into contact. The condition that the bodies do not penetrate is given
by

                                     𝑔(𝒙) = (𝒙 𝐵 − 𝒙¯ 𝐴 ).𝒏 𝐴 ≥ 0                            (2.24)

where 𝒙¯ 𝐴 is the point on Γ𝐶𝐴 that has the minimum distance to 𝒙 𝐵 . This point can be found by
a minimum distance problem provided the contact boundary describes a convex region (at least
locally). The outward normal 𝒏 𝐴 from body Ω 𝐴 was chosen arbitrarily. The non-penetration
condition Eq. 2.24 is also referred to as the gap function. The variation of this function is needed
to formulate weak forms of contact boundary value problems and is given here.

                                     𝛿𝑔(𝒙) = (𝛿𝒖 𝐵 − 𝛿 𝒖¯ 𝐴 ).𝒏 𝐴                            (2.25)

see [270] Section 4.3.1 for more details on this derivation.

The simplest form of frictionless contact enforces the purely geometrical non-penetration condition.
This is done by treating it as a constraint on displacements on the contact surface, leading to the
following contact (Karush-Kuhn-Tucker, or KKT) conditions

 𝑔(𝒙) ≥ 0
                                            𝜎𝑁 ≤ 0                                           (2.26)
 𝜎𝑁 𝑔(𝒙) = 0

where 𝜎𝑁 = 𝒏¯ 𝐴 .(𝝈. 𝒏¯ 𝐴 ). The second condition can be interpreted as enforcing that the contact
forces be only compressive, as no adhesion is considered. The final condition is the complementary
condition enforcing that when a gap exists (i.e. 𝑔(𝒙) > 0), the contact force 𝜎𝑁 is nonexistent, and
when in contact, there are non-zero forces but no gap (𝑔(𝒙) = 0).

 35

2.2.2. Contact Potential

When the contact interface is known, the space of admissible variations (those that do not self-
penetrate) remains the same as in the standard virtual work formulations (i.e. Eq. 2.4) and contact
can be enforced by adding additional terms to the virtual work equation rather than treating it as
a virtual inequality arising from the KKT conditions Eq. 2.26 1 . The most popular methods of
enforcing contact constraints in the engineering literature are penalty and Augmented Lagrangian
approaches, with the latter being favored for superior conditioning [214, 270, 213]. Penalty
approaches are popular in the optimization-based design literature as they naturally smooth out
the contact constraints and are easier to implement [180, 82, 166, 200, 259], although Augmented
Lagrangian approaches are also used [242, 95]. Penalty approaches don’t require the nested
iterations used to update Lagrange multipliers in the Augmented Lagrangian approach, making them
computationally more efficient. However, this comes at the expense of inexact contact enforcement
and possible conditioning issues. In the computer graphics community, barrier approaches have
become the standard, due in large part to the pioneering work in [169]. Like penalty methods,
barrier approaches don’t require nested iterations. In this work we formulate a contact potential
inspired by both barrier and penalty approaches. This contact potential is then added to the energy
functional in Eq. 2.2 so that the minimization approach and trust region solver described in Sections
2.1.2 and 2.1.4 can be directly employed.
In the barrier approach, a contact potential has the following general form

 ∫
                                             𝑊𝑐 = 𝜅          𝑏(𝑔 + (𝒙))𝑑Γ                                       (2.27)
 Γ 𝐴𝐵
where 𝜅 is a barrier parameter which plays a role in ensuring non-penetration and can be interpreted
as controlling the barrier stiffness. Typically, the parameter 𝜅 is adaptively updated in the barrier
contact algorithm. Here, 𝑔 + (𝒙) denotes that the non-penetration constraint is replaced by a gap that
is always positive, meaning that penetration can never occur. In [169], a smooth barrier function
is proposed with terms that are local for each potential contact pair, and is based on a distance
accuracy target parameter 𝑑ˆ > 0 that determines the maximum distance at which repulsive forces
will begin to occur. The smooth clamped barrier function is

 (  
 ˆ 2 ln
                                         −(𝑔(𝒙) − 𝑑)             𝑔(𝒙)
                                                                            for   0 < 𝑔(𝒙) < 𝑑ˆ
 ˆ =
                    𝑏(𝑔(𝒙), 𝑑)                                    𝑑ˆ                                            (2.28)
 0 for 𝑔(𝒙) ≥ 𝑑ˆ

which will only be evaluated for contact pairs where 𝑔(𝒙) ≤ 𝑑. ˆ With this barrier function,
the contact energy increases exponentially as the gap approaches 0, thus smoothly preventing
penetration.
Penalty approaches can be thought of in a similar manner to barrier approaches, but rather than
leaving a small positive gap between contacting bodies, they allow a small amount of penetration
 1 Note that this requires keeping and updating an active set of constraints throughout the solution process.  In this
 work we perform a nearest neighbor search for each segment in the contact set and update this neighbor list at each
 time step.

 36

(negative gap). A well known limitation of penalty approaches is their sensitivity to the value of the
penalty parameter. If this parameter is chosen is too small, an unacceptable amount of penetration
can occur. If it is too large, the system becomes poorly conditioned. In practice, this can lead to
trial-and-error manual tuning or ad-hoc update schemes, making it difficult to employ robustly for
optimization-based design. We instead propose a penalty potential inspired by the barrier function
in Eq. 2.28. Instead of increasing the contact energy exponentially as the gap approaches 0, we
allow a small amount of penetration and increase the energy exponentially as the gap approaches
this allowable value, i.e.

where 𝑑ˆ in this case is the maximum allowable penetration. For 𝑔(𝒙) = 0, the barrier function

With this barrier function, the contact potential we employ is

Unlike the barrier potential, this potential doesn’t require that gaps are always positive. As a result,
it can work for bodies that start in contact (gap of 0). The barrier function also helps to reduce
the need to calibrate a penalty value as it causes contact energy to increase exponentially as the
maximum allowable penetration is reached, but remain low if the gap is close to 0.
As contact edges slide in and out of contact, there is some non-smoothness in the energy response
that can result in discontinuous forces if not addressed. Strategies for smoothing these transitions
have been derived and implemented, but their details are beyond the scope of the current report.
The implementation details can be found in the OptimiSM code base [249].

2.2.3. Mortar Discretization

To discretize the contact surface for computation of gap values, the popular mortar method is used
[214, 270]. Mortar methods are often preferred over other approaches such as node-to-segment
methods as they provide enhanced stability, accuracy, and robustness by treating contact constraints
in a variationally consistent way that provides smoothing while passing patch tests [214, 91]. Mortar
methods start from a weak enforcement of the complementary condition Eq. 2.263 , i.e.

 ∫
                                                          𝜆𝑔(𝒙)𝑑Γ                                (2.31)
 Γ 𝐴𝐵

where 𝜆 was used instead of 𝜎𝑁 to allow for enforcement through Lagrange multiplier approaches.
Note that this term corresponds to the contact potential used in penalty approaches or Lagrange
multiplier approaches such as Augmented Lagrangian.

 37

The mortar segment-segment approach uses the finite element discretization of the surface Γ𝐶𝐵 as
the non-mortar surface over which integration is performed. This approach is also referred to as
standard Lagrange multiplier interpolation, in contrast to dual Lagrange multiplier interpolation
that makes use of dual approximating spaces (see e.g. [270, 91] for more details). The contact
pressure (not contact force in this case since it is within a spatial integral) is thus defined over the
non-mortar side by using the shape functions of the surface Γ𝐶𝐵 , i.e.

 ∑︁
                                               𝝀=          𝑁 𝐼𝐵 𝝀 𝐼                              (2.32)
 𝐼

The discrete contact energy (or discrete complementary condition) at node 𝐼 is thus

 ∫
                                         𝜆𝐼           𝑁 𝐼𝐵 𝑔(𝒙)𝑑Γ                                (2.33)
 Γ 𝐴𝐵

and the term in the integral can be seen as an averaged (smoothed) gap function. It now remains to
specify how the integral over the contact surface will be approximated. The two main approaches
are the segment-based and element-based approaches, but the focus here will be on the segment-
based approach. The reason for this is that the segment-based approach prevents discontinuities in
the integrand by creating smooth integrable segments. See [91] for a discussion on the pros and
cons of each approach.

Segment-based mortar integration approaches work with pairs of elements on the mortar and
non-mortar surfaces to define an intermediate surface over which numerical integration can be
performed. In three dimensions, the intermediate surface is constructed by projecting the nodes on
mortar and non-mortar segments (elements) onto the plane within which the non-mortar element
lies (referred to as an auxiliary plane), finding the polygon which contains the overlaps, triangulating
that polygon, and performing integration over the resulting triangles (see [214] for details). In two
dimensions, the process is simpler, and will be the focus in what follows. This approach was
originally laid out in [233] (see also [270] Section 8.4.1).

Figure 2-2 shows the segment-based approach for a two dimensional mesh where contact segments
are defined by the overlap between edges on the non-mortar and mortar surfaces. This overlap is
found by projecting nodes on the non-mortar edge (defined by points 𝒙1𝐵 and 𝒙2𝐵 ) onto the mortar
edge (defined by points 𝒙1𝐴 and 𝒙2𝐴 ) and vice versa using a normal vector. Typically, an averaged
normal vector 𝒏¯ = 𝒏 𝐴 −𝒏 𝐵 is utilized as it provides smoother behavior (see [214]). The intermediate
surface over which numerical integration is performed can then be taken as the segment defined by
the non-mortar side of the resulting quadrilateral or as the segment defined by the mortar side (or
some combination of both, see [233]). Typically, the non-mortar side is used and that will be the
assumption for the remainder of this section.
Once the points of the quadrilateral are know, gaps can be computed as 𝑔1 = (𝒙 1𝐵 − 𝒙¯ 𝐴 ).𝒏 and

using standard Gauss quadrature rules and treating the intermediate surface as a standard linear
edge element. Defining the parametric coordinate of this edge element as 𝜉, ˜ the discrete nodal
mortar projected gap function (i.e. the integrand in Eq. 2.33) can be approximated as

where 𝑛𝑔 𝑝 is the number of gauss points used in the quadrature evaluation, 𝑤 𝑔 the quadrature
weight, and 𝑗 𝑔 is the jacobian determinant of the mapping from parametric to physical space for the
intermediate surface. Shape functions on the intermediate surface edge element are denoted as 𝑁˜ 𝐽
and 𝑔𝐽 are the gap values at nodes of this element (i.e. 𝑔1 and 𝑔2 as defined above). The parametric
coordinate 𝜉𝑔𝐵 corresponds to the parametric coordinate in the non-mortar segment, and must be
computed as a function of the parametric coordinate 𝜉˜𝑔 in the intermediate surface edge element
for quadrature point 𝑔. This is done by simply interpolating the non-mortar segment parametric

where 𝜉 𝐽𝐵 , 𝐽 = 1, 2 is the parametric coordinate corresponding to points (𝒙1𝐵 and 𝒙¯ 𝐵 . Finally, a
remark should be made on the jacobian determinant 𝑗 𝑔 used in Eq. 2.34. This can simply be taken
as the jacobian determinant for the intermediate surface edge element, i.e. the length of the edge
element. It can also be computed as an average of smoothed edge segments of both the non-mortar
and mortar segments involved in constructing the intermediate surface edge element. The latter is
chosen in our implementation.
The above process is repeated for each of the closest mortar segments (found with standard contact
detection algorithms) to a given non-mortar segment and the contributions are summed into 𝑔 𝐵 .

3. MODELING THE RESPONSE OF SOFT METAMATERIALS

In this chapter we demonstrate how the energy minimization formulation and trust region solver
detailed in Section 2.1 can be used to robustly and directly compute energetic quantities in soft
metamaterials exploiting instabilities. We also demonstrate the use of the contact approach outlined
in Section 2.2 to capture widespread self contact occurring in soft metamaterials as they reach high
compressive strains. Finally, we show how the large configuration changes undergone by soft
metamaterials affect their linearized vibration response.
Unless otherwise stated, parameter values used in the viscoelastic material model are: equilibrium
shear modulus 𝐺 = 0.855 MPa, equilibrium bulk modulus 𝜅 = 855 MPa, non-equilibrium shear
modulus 𝐺 𝑛𝑒𝑞 = 3.42 MPa (4 times the equilibrium value), and relaxation time 𝜏 = 0.1 s. Fits
of different 3D printed elastomers have indicated the need for more than a single Maxwell branch
[87, 272], but the chosen properties are suitable for the purposes of demonstrating important rate-
dependent phenomena in soft metamaterials exhibiting instabilities, as will be illustrated with the
examples below. Examples that utilize a hyperelastic model employ a Neo-Hookean form with bulk
and shear moduli values equal to the equilibrium values in the viscoelastic model. Fully integrated
quadratic triangular plane-strain elements are also used throughout. While the equilibrium Poisson’s
ratio for the properties used is 0.4995, numerical experiments have indicated negligible locking
with the discretizations we employ. Finally, for the viscoelastic model the Weissenberg number
𝑊𝑖 = 𝜀𝜏¤ is used to characterize the different rate regimes, corresponding to an increase in strain rate
𝜀¤ as the relaxation time 𝜏 remains constant. Throughout this chapter, we choose values 𝑊𝑖 from a
range that covers responses between the extremes of rubbery and glassy response for elastomeric
materials. In this range, the interplay between viscoelastic relaxation and geometric instabilities
has a strong effect on the response of soft metamaterials, as is illustrated in the examples below.

3.1. Snap-Through Unit Cell

To begin, we consider a widely-studied design motif [220, 64, 228, 119, 51] with unit cells consisting
of thin angled beams attached to thicker components providing rigidity and guiding the deformation,
see Figure 3-1a, which shows the unit cell geometry and associated boundary conditions. Here,
horizontal degrees of freedom are fixed along the sides and vertical degrees of freedom fixed along
the bottom in order to mimic the placement of this unit cell within a periodic tessellation. While
periodic boundary conditions are not explicitly considered, the resulting boundary-value problem
sufficiently mimics the behavior of a unit cell for the purposes of demonstrating rate-dependent
snap-through phenomena.
First, the characteristic response of this unit cell is investigated using a hyperelastic material model
with the same Neo-Hookean form as the equilibrium strain energy in Eq. 2.12. Using displacement

 41

 (a) (b)

 Figure 3-1. Characteristic response of snap-through unit cell: (a) geometry and boundary-value
 problem with deformed configuration after instability superimposed, and (b) stress-strain
 response of a single snap-through unit cell compared to a finite chain of five cells, using a
 hyperelastic model.

control, the equilibrium path can be traced through the non-equilibrium transition that occurs during
snap-through as this portion is only unstable under traction control. This is done by applying a
maximum downward displacement of 65 mm over 65 equally-spaced time steps and using the
following trust region settings: Δ0 = 0.25, Δ𝑚𝑖𝑛 = 1 × 10−15 , 𝜀 = 5 × 10−8 , and a maximum number
of 500 trust region iterations. See Section 2.1.4 for details on solver parameters and how they fit into
the algorithm. The stress-strain response is shown in Figure 3-1b, which reveals the characteristic
snap-through response as a decrease in reaction force after reaching a critical point, followed by a
subsequent increase in force. Bi-stability can be identified for this unit cell geometry by noticing
that the reaction forces drop below 0.

This equilibrium path can be traced using root-finding approaches such as Newton’s method under
displacement control. It can also be traced using an arc-length approach. However, the equilibrium
path cannot be used to directly quantify the energy that gets dissipated through the non-equilibrium
transition occurring during snap-through. Instead, we must analyze the unit cell under traction
control. Here, a single loading cycle is considered by applying a force to the top surface (see
Figure 3-1a) over a time span corresponding to the strain rate used under displacement control
and then removing the force over the same time span. A maximum force of 1.25 N was applied
over 65 equally-spaced time steps and the following trust region settings were used: Δ0 = 2.0,
Δ𝑚𝑖𝑛 = 1 × 10−8 , 𝜀 = 1 × 10−8 , and a maximum number of 1100 trust region iterations. The
stress-strain response is also shown in Figure 3-1b where it can be seen to trace the equilibrium
path obtained under displacement control until reaching the critical point. The solution then snaps-
through the non-equilibrium transition to a configuration along the stable equilibrium path that can
support the increased load. Upon unloading, the traction control response again follows the stable
portion of the equilibrium path identified under displacement control. Bi-stability can be observed

 42

by noting that the displacement at which the applied force reaches 0 after unloading occurs at a
strain of about 0.3.
The jump in configuration that occurs during snap-through leads to a local release of strain energy
as it is converted into kinetic energy during the non-equilibrium transition. A duration of damped
elastic ringing about the new equilibrium configuration results in the dissipation of this kinetic
energy [220, 119, 96]. As mentioned in Section 2.1.1, the time scales involved make explicitly
modeling this dynamic process computationally intensive. However, the trust region solver provides
the robustness needed to make the large jump in configuration so that this can be modeled as a
quasi-static process. Hence, we can quantify the energy absorbed during snap-through just as one
would in an experiment, using the work performed over a cycle. The ability of the trust region
solver to robustly and directly compute these energetic quantities is a key differentiating factor when
compared to other nonlinear equation solvers. As described in Section 2.1.1, root-finding solvers
such as Newton’s method will struggle to make the jump in configuration, even when equipped
with line search. Arc length methods also cannot help in this case as they will trace the unstable
portion of the equilibrium path instead of making the jump in configuration.

3.2. Finite Chain of Unit Cells

Next, the response of a finite metamaterial sample is analyzed. To do so, we consider uniaxial
compression of a chain of five unit cells arranged in series under displacement control. The
boundary conditions utilized for the five cell chain are the same as those shown in Figure 3-1a
for a single unit cell, and a maximum downward displacement of 350 mm is applied over 350
equally-spaced time steps. Trust region settings are the same as described above for the single cell
under traction control. Figure 3-1b also shows the stress-strain response of this finite chain of cells.
The response tracks the stable portions of the single cell curve, but contains intermediate drops in
force corresponding to discrete snap-through events of each unit cell in the chain, resulting in a
serrated stress-strain curve. With each snap-through event, the stress at the bottom of the unstable
portion of the equilibrium path increases progressively, and given enough unit cells would converge
to the curve obtained for a single cell under traction control [96].
The serrated stress-strain curve in Figure 3-1b highlights the energy dissipating mechanisms at
play in the finite chain. As individual unit cells undergo a sequence of snap-through events under
displacement control, the non-equilibrium transitions result in a local release of strain energy. This
is illustrated in Figure 3-2, which shows the strain energy distribution in the deformed configuration
at different points. Comparing the strain energy distributions before and after snap-through events
(e.g. between Figure 3-2 (a) and (b), (c) and (d), and (e) and (f)) reveals a significant amount of
energy being released as deformation localizes in the unit cell undergoing snap-through.
Following the equilibrium path through the unstable portion that occurs during these snap-through
events (with, e.g., an arc-length solver) would reveal snap-back behavior as both the load and
displacement increments decrease. This snap-back occurs due to interactions between unit cells
and can’t be captured by modeling a single cell [220, 96]. Again, the trust region solver is able to
make the jump in configuration over these unstable portions, allowing us to directly compute the
energy released through such non-equilibrium transitions.

 43

 Figure 3-2. Strain energy density (MPa) distribution in deformed configuration for a chain of 5
 snap-through unit cells using a hyperelastic model: (a)-(b) before and after the first snap-through
event, (c)-(d) before and after the third snap-through event, (e)-(f) before and after the fifth and final
 snap-through event.

 44

3.3. Interplay Between Viscoelasticity and Snapping Instabilities

 (a) (b)

Figure 3-3. Rate-dependent force-displacement response of snap-through unit cell: (a) single unit
 cell response, and (b) response of a finite chain of five cells.

We now employ the viscoelastic model to study how the interplay between viscoelastic relaxation
and snap-through instabilities changes the response of both a single unit cell and the five cell
chain. Beginning with a single unit cell, we use displacement control to highlight how the entire
equilibrium path gets altered by viscoelastic effects. The force-displacement response at different
values of Weissenberg number 𝑊𝑖 is shown in Figure 3-3a. At sufficiently low values of 𝑊𝑖 the
response shows no viscous effects, while increasing 𝑊𝑖 causes an increase in the initial stiffness and
critical load. The reaction forces on the unstable portion of the equilibrium path also increase with
increasing rate, to the point where bi-stability is apparently lost (reaction forces on the unstable
portion never drop below 0) using a Weissenberg number 𝑊𝑖 = 1 × 10−2 . Such a rather non-intuitive
trend follows those observed using a simplified truss model with viscoelastic springs in [88].

The response of the five cell chain is shown in Figure 3-3b and generally follows the same trend as
the single unit cell. That is, the critical points at the peaks of the curves shift to both a higher load
and a higher displacement as the Weissenberg number 𝑊𝑖 increases. At higher values of 𝑊𝑖 (i.e.
𝑊𝑖 = 1 × 10−3 or higher), the overall shape of the force-displacement curve changes substantially,
with the initial peak becoming much larger in comparison to subsequent peaks. Additionally,
the drop-offs after each peak are no longer as sharp, indicating that the jumps in configuration
undergone by individual unit cells are reduced.

Figure 3-4 shows the strain energy distribution at different points before and after snap-through
events in the deformed configuration with 𝑊𝑖 = 1 × 10−2 . Here, a markedly different response
is observed as compared to Figure 3-2. Due to viscoelastic effects becoming more prominent,
the strain energy is far less localized and the release of strain energy is less pronounced. Indeed,
before the first snap-through event all five cells display deformation in their ligaments and this
energy remains mostly stored after the snap-through event. The release of strain energy is instead
observed more acutely in the bulky columns and base of the unit cell. This trend holds for all three
snap-through events displayed.

  Figure 3-4. Strain energy density (MPa) distribution in deformed configuration for a chain of 5
snap-through unit cells using a viscoelastic model and Weissenberg number 𝑊𝑖 = 1 × 10−2 : (a)-(b)
before and after the first snap-through event, (c)-(d) before and after the third snap-through event,
 (e)-(f) before and after the fifth and final snap-through event.

3.4. Buckling Instabilities

A widely studied design motif for soft metamaterials exploiting buckling instabilities to undergo
programmable pattern transformations is a square array of circular pores [195, 34, 230, 207, 186].
Such pattern transformations are enabled by bifurcation of the equilibrium path leading to new
configurations as symmetry is broken, rather than by exploiting a large jump in configuration along
a principal branch. Dealing with bifurcations is a challenge for root-finding algorithms because
they have no intrinsic way of distinguishing between stable and unstable configurations. Indeed,
root-finding algorithms will typically remain on the principal branch even as it becomes unstable
past a bifurcation point [31, 248]. Figure 2-1 helps to demonstrate why. Here, the force level
at points 𝐶 and 𝐸 is identical, and for an applied force equal (or approximately equal) to this
value, both configurations are roots to the residual force equation. However, only point 𝐸 is a
stable equilibrium position under this force. The configuration at 𝐶 is unstable, meaning that
no real structure would come to rest in this configuration. Additional criteria must be brought
into the root search to discern stable configurations from such spurious unstable ones. Common
approaches include tracking the eigenvalues of the tangent stiffness matrix to apply branch switching
or superimposing imperfections in the form of linear elastic buckling modes. In contrast, the trust
region solver can inherently distinguish between stable and unstable configurations in order to
seek out stable energy-minimizing equilibrium configurations. Once again, the trust region solver
allows us to directly compute desired quantities of interest without introducing ad-hoc numerical
approaches that complicate the design optimization process.

To demonstrate the trust region solver’s ability to identify stable energy-minimizing equilibrium
paths in the presence of bifurcations while accounting for viscoelastic effects, we analyze a 5x5
array of pores under uniaxial compression. The pores have a diameter of 8.7 mm and center-to-
center spacing of 10 mm, resulting in a porosity close to 60 %. Figure 3-5a shows the geometry
and boundary conditions, where degrees of freedom on the top surface are subject to a uniform
displacement in the vertical direction while being fixed in the horizontal direction and degrees
of freedom on the bottom surface are fixed in both directions. A single loading-unloading cycle
applying a maximum downward displacement of 5 mm (10 % strain) is considered using 80 equally
spaced time steps. Trust region settings used for this problem are: Δ0 = 0.25, Δ𝑚𝑖𝑛 = 1 × 10−15 ,
𝜀 = 5 × 10−8 , and a maximum number of 100 trust region iterations.

Figure 3-5b shows the force-displacement response for a range of Weissenberg numbers. It can be
seen that an increase in Weissenberg number results in increased hysteresis generated through vis-
coelastic relaxation. Additionally, an increase in the Weissenberg number brings about an increase
in both the load and displacement at which the critical point occurs. At a high enough Weissenberg
number (𝑊𝑖 = 1.0 × 10−2 ), this increase in critical displacement is enough to suppress bifurcation
altogether within the displacement range considered. This behavior was noted experimentally in
[87], where a similar geometry (periodic array of voids) was studied, and results from viscous ef-
fects causing a time delay between the applied displacement and instability-induced configuration
change. A similar phenomenon was remarked on in [272], where the bifurcation behavior of a
periodic set of circular inclusions was studied numerically and experimentally.

 47

 (a) (b)

Figure 3-5. Rate-dependent response of buckling metamaterial: (a) geometry and boundary-value
 problem with deformed configuration after instability superimposed, and (b) force-displacement
 response.

3.5. Self Contact

 (a) (b)

  Figure 3-6. Self-contact response of buckling metamaterial: (a) deformed configuration at 30 %
 strain, and (b) force-displacement response.

Due to their porous nature, soft metamaterials can experience widespread self-contact as they are
subject to large compressive strains. We utilize two soft metamaterial design motifs to demonstrate
how the contact approach outlined in Section 2.2 can be used to capture this response. In the below
examples, the contact settings are as follows: the maximum allowable penetration 𝑑ˆ is set to 20 %
of the length of the thinnest ligament and the barrier stiffness 𝜅 is given a value of 5.
The first design motif studied is the square array of circular pores from Section 3.4. Figure 3-6
shows the deformed configuration and force-displacement response when the maximum downward

 48

displacement is increased to 15 mm (30 % strain) using 120 equally spaced time steps. Here,
widespread self-contact can be observed as the pores collapse, with the resulting increase in
stiffness visible in the force-displacement response. We next consider the response of a square
truss lattice unit cell with vertical and diagonal struts, shown in Figure 3-7a. The cell length is 10
mm, and strut diameter is 1 mm. We subject this cell to a downward displacement of 5 mm (50
% strain) applied on the top surface over 100 equally spaced time steps while the bottom surface
remains fixed. Figure 3-7b shown the force-displacement response with the deformed configuration
in the insert. Again, from the force-displacement response we can see the characteristic response
of soft metamaterials at high compressive strains: a linear initial response followed by the onset
of buckling, a relatively stable post-buckling plateau, and finally a rapid stiffening as self-contact
occurs and the metamaterial densifies.

 (a) (b)

 Figure 3-7. Self-contact response of lattice cell: (a) undeformed geometry, and (b)
 force-displacement response with deformed configuration at 50 % strain in the insert.

3.6. Linearized Vibration Response

One of the appeals of soft metamaterials is that the large configuration changes they undergo can
lead to a vastly different linearized vibration response, allowing for different frequency ranges
or directions to be damped depending on the preload level [264, 286]. We can assess the effect
of preload on the linearized vibration response of soft metamaterials by solving the generalized
eigenvalue problem of the form

                                          𝑩(𝒖)v𝑖 = 𝜆𝑖 Mv𝑖 ,                                      (3.1)
where 𝑩(𝒖) is the tangent stiffness matrix at a given equilibrium configuration 𝒖 (Eq. 2.8), M is the
mass matrix, 𝜆𝑖 are the squares of the natural (circular) frequencies, and v𝑖 are the corresponding
eigenmodes.
Cui and Harne [66] explore the effect of preload on the vibration response of a simple soft
metamaterial design consisting of an array of rectangular pores. By using a simple geometry that

 49

allows them to control the mechanisms of nonlinearity, they are able to create an analytic spring
model that enables deeper exploration of the interplay between nonlinear elasticity, viscous effects,
and bifurcations. For our purposes, this geometry provides a way to verify that we can qualitatively
produce experimentally-measured vibration responses about preload states that include critical
points.

 (a) (b)

 (c) (d)

 Figure 3-8. Effect of preload on vibration response in soft metamaterials: (a) snapshots of
  metamaterial sample at different levels of preload (from [66]), (b) deformed configurations at
  different levels of preload, (c) force transmissibility and frequency at different preload levels
measured experimentally in [66], (d) evolution of simulated lowest natural frequency and reaction
 force.

Figure 3-8 compares the experimental results from [66] to results obtained by simulating the
same metamaterial sample. First, the deformed configurations at different levels of preload are
compared. Figure 3-8a shows snapshots from experiments carried out in [66] with the metamaterial
sample uncompressed, near the critical point, just past the critical point, and well into the post-
buckled regime. Snapshots of the deformed configuration obtained by simulating this metamaterial

 50

sample are shown in Figure 3-8b, where it can be seen that they agree closely. Figure 3-8c shows
experimental force transmissibility results from [66]. From this, we can develop an understanding
of how the resonance frequency of the metamaterial sample changes as a function of preload. As
the critical point is approached, this frequency shifts lower, but after traversing the critical point
it shifts to a higher frequency than in the pre-buckled state. Figure 3-8d plots the evolution of the
simulated lowest natural frequency as a function of the simulation time step. The evolution of the
reaction force is also plotted so that the simulation time steps corresponding to the pre-buckling
and post-buckling regimes can be observed. From this, it can be seen that the simulation follows
the trend observed experimentally in [66]. That is, the frequency initially shifts downward as the
critical point is approached and then quickly increases past the initial frequency as the sample enters
the post-buckled configuration.

 51

 52

4. OPTIMIZATION-BASED DESIGN APPROACH

In this chapter we detail the optimization-based design framework developed to provide smooth
mappings from geometric and local material design parameters to useful quantities that can be
computed directly by the trust region solver. These quantities can then be used to drive the
nonlinear response of soft metamaterials by solving a PDE-constrained optimization problem.

4.1. Geometric Design

Our approach to geometric design entails two different mappings, which are illustrated in Figure
4-1:
 1. A geometry module maps a set of design parameters 𝝓 to a conformal finite element mesh
 of the geometric domain Ω𝑎 ( 𝑿) defined by nodal coordinates 𝑿 (𝝓).
 2. A physics module maps the conformal mesh Ω𝑎 ( 𝑿) to the scalar quantities 𝑓 ( 𝑿) that can be
 used to tailor the nonlinear response of a design.
Gradient-based optimization is enabled by computing derivatives of the scalar quantities 𝑓 ( 𝑿 (𝝓))
with respect to the design parameters 𝝓, i.e. 𝑑𝑓 /𝑑𝝓. This derivative is obtained by applying the
chain rule to compose the derivatives of each individual mapping — i.e. 𝑑𝑓 /𝑑 𝑿 and 𝑑 𝑿/𝑑𝝓 —
which are computed by the physics module and geometry module, respectively. To orchestrate
this workflow and control the flow of data between the different modules, we employ the Plato
optimization-based design engine [123] developed at Sandia National Laboratories.
Chapters 5 and 6 provide details on the different mappings used for shape and topology opti-
mization, respectively, and how they are differentiated, but we first remark on the choice of using a
remeshing approach in our framework. The reasons for this choice are two-fold. First, this approach
accommodates large shape changes while avoiding drawbacks of fixed mesh approaches such as
the need for elaborate interpolation and penalization rules [282, 70] or immersed approaches which
can suffer from ill-conditioning [73]. Second, this approach is flexible enough to be used with
different design parameterizations for topology optimization using level-sets [12, 94, 226] or shape
optimization with CAD parameters. We demonstrate the latter in Chapter 5 before covering the
the former in Chapter 6. Drawbacks of the remeshing approach include increased computational
time to generate an analysis model and a lack of robustness in meshing software for complex
geometries. The former is less of a concern for nonlinear problems where the computational cost
is overwhelmingly driven by the finite element solver. Although, for large-scale three-dimensional
problems that require massively parallel implementations remeshing may prove to be a bottleneck.
The latter remains a concern, but meshing software is becoming increasingly robust, and there were
no meshing issues with any of the design problems considered in this study. Using a remeshing

 53

 𝑑𝑓 𝑑𝑓 𝑑𝑿

Figure 4-1. Flow of data within optimization framework. Orchestration of data flow is performed by
 the Plato Engine [123].

approach also introduces discretization errors into the gradient computation, but these are less of a
concern for optimization-based design, see the discussion in Appendix B, Section B.1.

4.2. Material Layout Design

Optimization-based design approaches can also be applied to generate spatially-varying material
layouts in geometries that are unchanging. Such an approach will be demonstrated in Chapter 7,
where a grayscale Digital Light Processing (DLP) printing technique is used to realize intricate
layouts of varied material properties within components. In this case there is no geometry module,
as the geometry is meshed once at the beginning of the workflow and the same mesh is used
throughout. Instead of geometric parameters, the design variables 𝝓 are a field defined on the mesh
that is used to determine local material properties. Thus, there is only one mapping:

 1. A physics module maps the set of design parameters 𝝓 to the scalar quantities 𝑓 (𝝓) that can
 be used to tailor the nonlinear response of a design.

Derivatives of the scalar quantities 𝑓 (𝝓) with respect to the design parameters 𝝓, i.e. 𝑑𝑓 /𝑑𝝓, are
computed directly by the physics module.

 54

4.3. Scalar Quantities of Interest

OptimiSM [249] is employed as the physics module so that the trust region solver presented in
Chapter 2 Section 2.1 can be used to robustly and directly perform the mappings from a conformal
finite element mesh Ω𝑎 ( 𝑿) (for geometric design) or design variable field 𝝓 (for material layout
design) to the scalar quantities 𝑓 ( 𝑿). For each mesh Ω𝑎 ( 𝑿) or design variable field 𝝓, the trust
region solver computes the equilibrium displacement field 𝒖 𝑘 at every time 𝑡 𝑘 , and this field is used
to compute scalar quantities of interest.

4.3.1. Nonlinear Response

Several different quantities 𝑓 (𝝓) are considered in order to tailor the nonlinear response of soft
metamaterials. The first considers direct tailoring of the force-displacement response by minimizing
the L2 norm of the difference between points on the curve and a finite set of target points (i.e., the
“direct" approach discussed in [46], see also [171, 187]). The particular form we adopt is:

 v

                                                    ∑︁ (𝐹𝑖 − 𝐹ˆ𝑖 ) 2

where 𝐹𝑖 are reaction force values and 𝐹ˆ𝑖 target force values at one of the 𝑛ˆ points chosen along the
force-displacement curve.
Other objectives aim to tailor the nonlinear response indirectly by considering energetic quantities.
For instance, the area under a force-displacement curve can be related to energy absorption and
quantified as the total external work done along the equilibrium path. In this study, we approximate
this integral using the trapezoid rule, i.e.

 ∫ 𝑢¯ 𝑛 𝑛
