Section 5.2.1) it can be beneficial to consider instead the complementary work

which has advantages for stiffness design when considering geometric nonlinearities [49]. Here 𝐹¯ 𝑛
is the final applied force.
Additionally, it is useful to characterize how much of the work done on a metamaterial sample gets
dissipated through geometric instabilities and/or viscoelastic material response. While the total

 55

dissipated energy occurring through the viscoelastic material response can be directly utilized [282,
283], this quantity requires a large number of time steps to be properly resolved (see Appendix A).
Importantly for the consideration of soft metamaterials, this quantity also doesn’t take into account
the energy that is dissipated through non-equilibrium transitions (see the discussion in Section 3.1).
Instead, we can quantify the energy dissipated through these non-equilibrium transitions as the
difference between total external work and strain energy in the metamaterial. When viscoelastic
material effects are present, they are also accounted in this difference. Hence, a measure of
dissipated energy utilized herein is

 𝑓𝑑𝑖𝑠𝑠 ≈ 𝑓𝑤𝑜𝑟 𝑘 − 𝜓 𝑛 (4.4)
where 𝜓 𝑛 is the strain energy at the final time step. For the viscoelastic model, 𝜓 𝑛 is the sum of
 𝑛 𝑛
both equilibrium and non-equilibrium energies, i.e. 𝜓 𝑛 = 𝜓 𝑒𝑞 + 𝜓 𝑛𝑒𝑞 .
Finally, for energy absorption applications another useful quantity is the crush force efficiency
(CFE). This can be defined as the ratio between the average reaction force and the peak reaction
force, which relates roughly to a measure of how flat the post-buckling plateau remains [26]. The
flatter this plateau is, the more the response of a metamaterial resembles an ideal energy absorber.
We define this quantity as

 1 𝑓𝑤𝑜𝑟 𝑘
                                          𝑓𝑐 𝑓 𝑒 =                                            (4.5)
 𝐹 𝑛 𝑢𝑛
where the average reaction force is computed as the total work 𝑓𝑤𝑜𝑟 𝑘 divided by the final applied
displacement 𝑢 𝑛 , and the final reaction force 𝐹 𝑛 is used in place of the peak reaction force.
This definition restricts the use of this quantity to problems analyzed under displacement control.
Additionally, the assumption that the final reaction force can be used to replace the peak reaction
force does not hold in general as softening can occur post-buckling. However, for problems with
self contact, this assumption is reasonable.

4.3.2. Linearized Response

As discussed in Section 3.6, the large configuration changes undergone by soft metamaterials can
lead to large differences in the linearized vibration response before and after preload. Thus, we aim
to consider scalar quantities 𝑓 (𝝓) that can be used to tailor the linearized vibration response about
a preloaded equilibrium configuration. Such an idea was explored in [68], where optimization-
based design was carried out to design periodic metamaterial unit cells that maximize a frequency
bandgap in the deformed configuration, while minimizing it in the undeformed configuration. We
instead aim to control how much the natural frequencies change when a finite metamaterial sample
undergoes preload. To do so, we take the difference between the lowest eigenvalue in the deformed
and undeformed configurations as our quantity of interest, i.e.

                                           𝑓𝜆 = ∥𝜆𝑛0 − 𝜆00 ∥ 2                                    (4.6)
Here, 𝜆00 and 𝜆𝑛0 are the lowest eigenvalues in the undeformed and deformed configurations, respec-
tively (see Eq. 3.1).

 56

4.4. Adjoint Sensitivity Analysis

Derivatives of the mapping from Ω𝑎 ( 𝑿) to the scalar quantities 𝑓 ( 𝑿) for geometric design and
from 𝝓 to 𝑓 (𝝓) for material layout design are computed in OptimiSM using an analytic adjoint
approach that is modified to leverage the automatic differentiation capabilities of Jax [42]. We
follow the general path-dependent adjoint approach laid out in [7] and write the inverse problem
of finding an optimized set of design parameters 𝝓 that minimize a given quantity of interest 𝑓 (𝝓)
as

 min 𝑓 (𝝓),
 𝝓

                       s.t.   𝑹 𝑘 (𝝓, 𝒖ˆ 𝑘 , 𝒖ˆ 𝑘−1 , 𝒄 𝑘 , 𝒄 𝑘−1 ) = 0,    𝑘 = 1, 2, ..., 𝑛,     (4.7)
 𝑘 𝑘 𝑘−1 𝑘 𝑘−1
                              𝑯 (𝝓, 𝒖ˆ , 𝒖ˆ          ,𝒄 ,𝒄         ) = 0,   𝑘 = 1, 2, ..., 𝑛,
 0 ≤ 𝝓 ≤ 1.
Here, design parameters 𝝓 are constrained to be within bounds of 0 to 1 to ensure normalization
within the optimizer and then rescaled to physically-meaningful values when used to generate the
CAD geometry. Eq. 4.7 is a general form for optimization problems constrained by PDEs that
govern the evolution of a state over a number of discrete time steps 𝑘 [188, 7]. The discretized form
of the governing PDE is 𝑹 𝑘 = 0, while 𝑯 𝑘 = 0 represents a discrete set of local equations which
also constrain the state. We define the state using a set of global variables 𝒖ˆ which correspond to
the primary variables of the governing PDE and local variables 𝒄 which implicitly constrain the
state.

In this work, we are concerned with PDEs that govern the equilibrium path of viscoelastic or
elastic solids. As such, the set of unconstrained nodal displacements 𝒖 serves as the state 𝒖. ˆ
For viscoelastic materials, the set of integration point level internal variables (i.e. the inelastic
deformation gradient 𝑭 𝑣 at each integration point, see Section 2.1.3) serves as local variables 𝒄.
The discretized PDE constraint 𝑹 𝑘 = 0 is thus the condition Eq. 2.6, while the local equations
𝑯 𝑘 = 0 are the evolution equations for internal variables 𝑭 𝑣 at the integration point level. For
the case of elastic solids, there is no dependence on 𝒄 and no local equations 𝑯 𝑘 = 0, greatly
simplifying the adjoint derivation. In the below derivation we will focus on the case of viscoelastic
solids, as this represents the more complex case.

The PDE-constrained optimization problem Eq. 4.7 is solved using the reduced-space or nested
approach wherein the state is assumed to be an implicit function of the design parameters and the
PDE equality constraints are strictly enforced. With this nested approach, PDE constraints are
satisfied by solving the forward FEA problem for the state given the design parameters 𝝓 at each
optimization iteration, and this state is used to evaluate the objective function 𝑓 (𝝓). Recall that the
condition Eq. 2.6 is the discrete form of the necessary condition for the minimization problem Eq.
2.1 to be satisfied. Hence, in the nested approach the PDE constraints in Eq. 4.7 are satisfied by
using the trust region algorithm in OptimiSM to solve the minimization problem Eq. 2.1.

The general path-dependent adjoint approach in [7] accounts for the implicit constraints 𝑹 𝑘 = 0
and 𝑯 𝑘 = 0 at each time step 𝑘 by forming the following Lagrangian function

the adjoint approach to eliminate the implicit derivative 𝑑 𝒖ˆ 𝑘 /𝑑𝝓. This allows us to write the global

4.5. Optimization Algorithms

As discussed in Section 4.4, a nested approach is used to handle the PDE-constraints in Eq. 4.7.
The resulting bound-constrained optimization problem is then solved using a Kelley-Sachs trust
region algorithm [149] implemented in the Rapid Optimization Library (ROL) [155]. Optimization
is terminated when the gradient norm is below 1 × 10−6 , the change in design parameters is less
than 1 × 10−14 , or when a maximum number of trust region iterations — set to 50 unless otherwise
specified — is exceeded. In practice, these convergence tolerances prove to be quite strict and
termination is often caused by exceeding the maximum number of iterations. The convergence
plots given in the following chapters show that most problems are acceptably converged.
In Chapter 6, a nonlinear constraint in included in the problem formulation. To solve the resulting
nonlinear inequality-constrained problem we use the sequential quadratic programming algorithm
as implemented in the SNOPT package [110]. Optimization is terminated when the major optimality
tolerance is below 1×10−8 and the major feasibility tolerance is below 1×10−2 , or when a maximum
number of major iterations — set to 250 unless otherwise specified — is exceeded.

 61

 62

5. AN OPTIMIZATION-BASED APPROACH TO TAILOR THE
 MECHANICAL RESPONSE OF SOFT METAMATERIALS
 UNDERGOING RATE-DEPENDENT INSTABILITIES

The vast majority of the works discussed in Section 1.3 have focused on the design of soft meta-
materials whose constitutive behavior is modeled as elastic, i.e. exhibiting no dissipative effects.
However, several recent studies have highlighted how viscoelastic effects can non-intuitively alter
the emergence of instabilities in soft metamaterials. Varying the strain rate applied to soft metama-
terials can shift the critical load at which instabilities occur in a non-monotonic manner, result in
the emergence of different buckling patterns, and suppress the emergence of instabilities altogether
[87, 272]. As many elastomers used in AM exhibit non-negligible viscoelastic relaxation, this be-
havior has a large influence on the design of soft metamaterials for applications where the loading
rate is non-trivial. Moreover, exploiting the interplay between viscous relaxation and geometric
instabilities can lead to new avenues for soft metamaterials design including extreme hysteresis and
rate-dependent configurational switching [140, 88].
While some of the recent works employing machine learning have considered the inclusion of
inelastic constitutive behavior when generating training data, there is a clear lack of optimization-
based design approaches that can combine geometric instabilities and material inelasticity. In this
chapter, we introduce an optimization-based design approach for tailoring the response of soft
metamaterials, exploiting both geometric instabilities and viscoelastic relaxation. Relevant to the
present contribution are some studies which have explored topology optimization of structures and
materials exhibiting large deformation viscoelastic effects [282, 283], but instabilities were not the
design focus therein. Our contribution thus addresses a gap when it comes to optimization-based
design approaches for controlling the inelastic response of structures undergoing instabilities.
The effectiveness of our optimization-based design approach is illustrated by carrying out CAD-
based shape optimization of finite-sized metamaterial samples consisting of structural motifs that
are simple, manufacturable, and yet rich in structural responses. Examples are chosen to highlight
the novel and useful responses which can be elicited from soft metamaterials due to the unique
features of our approach. For instance, we control the energy dissipation due to both material and
geometric effects, the elastic energy stored in the structure, the structural transitions from bi-stable
to mono-stable responses, and the peak force transmissible by the structure (i.e., mechanical fuse
behavior).

5.1. CAD-parameterized Shape Optimization

In this chapter the Engineering Sketch Pad (ESP) [120] is employed as the geometry module (see
Figure 4-1) so that CAD-parameterized shape optimization can be performed. ESP is a feature-

Figure 5-1. Illustration of mapping from a single design parameter 𝜙 to a conformal finite element
mesh Ω𝑎 ( 𝑿 (𝜙)) using ESP. The geometry is defined as a parameterized feature tree so that a new
geometry is generated for each value of 𝜙. Meshing this geometry provides Ω𝑎 ( 𝑿 (𝜙)), illustrated
 here for two different values of 𝜙.

 64

based parametric solid modeling package built on OpenCASCADE, EGADS, and OpenCSM
[121, 120]. It was developed to enable the creation and manipulation of geometry with the goal
of supporting computational analysis tools. As such, geometry models created using ESP are
guaranteed to be water-tight and mesh-able. They are also parametric, being defined in terms of
a feature tree dependent on a set of user-defined parameters 𝝓 that can be varied to facilitate the
generation of new geometries. Conformal meshes of the 2D geometries employed in this study
are generated as unstructured 2D triangular grids using the Advancing-Front/Local-Reconnection
(AFLR) procedure [182] through the AFLR2 analysis interface module (AIM) provided in ESP’s
Computational Aircraft Prototype Syntheses (CAPS) ecosystem. Hence, we use ESP to obtain the
mappings from design parameters 𝝓 to a conformal finite element mesh Ω𝑎 ( 𝑿). This is illustrated
in Figure 5-1 for a simple geometry with a single design parameter 𝜙. Crucially, ESP also offers
the unique capability of computing sensitivities of components of a geometric configuration (i.e.
faces, edges, vertices) with respect to these design parameters. These sensitivities can then be used
to obtain sensitivities of grid points defined on the geometry by the AFLR2 mesh generator [71],
providing the necessary derivatives 𝑑 𝑿/𝑑𝝓. Such tight integration between CAD geometry, design
parameters, and mesh generation – when coupled through the Plato Engine to sensitivities computed
by a physics module – achieves the vision of a robust shape optimization module recommended in
the review [250].

5.2. Design of Snap-Through Metamaterials

In this section, the unit cell geometry studied in Section 3.1 is considered to illustrate shape
optimization of soft metamaterials exploiting snap-through and snap-back instabilities. Shape
optimization is carried out on design domains consisting of a single unit cell and a five cell chain,
with boundary conditions as illustrated in Figure 3-1a and described in Section 3.2. The trust region
solver settings are the same as described in Chapter 3, but a maximum number of 2500 trust region
iterations is used to ensure solver success during optimization. Figure 5-2a shows the geometric
parameters and Figure 5-2b the nominal parameter values of a unit cell, adapted from the design
studied in [51]. Finally, we report the optimized design parameter values for each of the examples
in Appendix C.

5.2.1. Example 1: Maximizing Energy Absorption Capacity Using a Single Unit
 Cell

Quantifying the energy dissipation for a single unit cell can provide an upper bound on a finite-sized
metamaterial sample, and hence is often termed the theoretical energy absorption capacity [96].
As discussed in Section 3.2, if enough unit cells are arranged in series (usually on the order of
dozens to hundreds [220, 119]), their response converges to the response of a single unit cell under
traction control. We showed in Section 3.1 that the ability of the trust region solver to capture large
jumps in configuration allows us to directly compute this traction control response, in contrast to
root-finding solvers employing Newton’s method or arc-length continuation. Hence, we can use
our optimization-based design framework to directly maximize the theoretical energy absorption
capacity of this unit cell.

  Figure 5-3. Results of snap-through unit cell optimization under traction control with maximum

divide the complementary work quantity by the displacement value obtained at the final time step.
The resulting quantity is termed complementary force.

Figure 5-3b shows the force-displacement response of the initial design using the nominal geometric
parameter values in Figure 5-2b as well as the response of designs obtained by minimizing the
complementary work and the complementary force under a maximum applied force 𝐹¯ = 3 N. Here,
a hyperelastic material model is employed so as to focus solely on the energy absorption occurring
from non-equilibrium transitions. It can be seen that minimizing the complementary work raises
the force at which snap-through occurs in comparison to the nominal design, but also reduces
the displacement undergone during snap-through. Minimizing the complementary force, however,
increases both of these quantities, thus leading to greater theoretical energy absorption.

 (a) (b)

Figure 5-4. Results of snap-through unit cell optimization using the complementary force objective
  under different values of maximum applied force 𝐹: ¯ (a) using a hyperelastic model, (b) using a
 viscoelastic model and Weissenberg number 𝑊𝑖 = 1 × 10−3 .

 67

The complementary force objective favors designs with snap-through occurring right at the max-
imum applied force, as this promotes large displacements from snap-through while still reducing
the complementary work. To demonstrate this, Figure 5-4 shows the optimized force-displacement
responses obtained using the complementary force objective to carry out optimization for a range
of different maximum applied forces 𝐹. ¯ Here, both the hyperelastic model and the viscoelastic
model with Weissenberg number 𝑊𝑖 = 1 × 10−3 are considered. It can be seen that the force at
which snap-through initiates is right around the maximum applied force. Comparing the optimized
design parameter values in Tables C-2 and C-3 obtained using the different constitutive models
under the same maximum applied force, it can be seen that different designs are obtained as the
substantial rate effects occurring at a Weissenberg number 𝑊𝑖 = 1 × 10−3 are accounted for by the
optimizer.

This example demonstrates the utility of the complementary force objective for tailoring the snap-
through response of a unit cell so as to maximize the theoretical energy absorption capacity
under a specific applied force. This is enabled by the robustness of the trust region solver when
dealing with large configurational changes, enabling us to directly and robustly compute the traction
control response. However, even with a robust forward problem solver, careful thought must be
given to formulating optimization-based design problems that are well-posed in the face of the
large configuration changes (and thus jumps in the force-displacement response) undergone during
snap-through. Indeed, we observed that non-convexity in the complementary force objective
landscape caused the Kelley-Sachs trust region algorithm used for shape optimization to struggle
occasionally. Because of this, a maximum of 100 trust region iterations was specified for terminating
this algorithm when the complementary force objective was used. This need for more iterations
can be seen in the convergence plot in Figure 5-3a. Additionally, starting designs with parameter
values differing from the nominal values were sometimes needed to obtain designs with critical
points occurring at the maximum applied force. We leave it as the subject of future research to
investigate different possible problem formulations, optimization algorithms, and settings which
may ameliorate this strong dependence on initial design.

5.2.2. Example 2: Maximizing Energy Dissipation by Grading a Chain of Cells

Often, analytic models are used to study the behavior of chains of snap-through unit cells [220,
119, 51] and explore how changes in model parameters relate to overall behavioral changes of the
metamaterial. Such low-fidelity models are useful for a coarse bounding of a design space, but
are of limited use for optimizing performance due to the complex interplay between unit cells with
different geometric features. Here, we directly modify geometric parameters of a finite chain of
five cells to tailor the response for maximum energy dissipation. We showed in Section 3.2 that
large jumps in configuration undergone by individual cells in a finite chain cause a release of strain
energy that manifests as snap-back response under displacement control. Again, the ability of the
trust region solver to robustly handle these jumps in configuration allows us to directly compute the
energy dissipated through such non-equilibrium transitions for use in optimization-based design.
We do so by taking difference between the total work and the strain energy, i.e. we maximize the
dissipated energy Eq. 4.4.

 68

The design parameters are chosen to be the apex heights ℎ of each unit cell in the five cell chain,
resulting in a set of 5 design parameters 𝝓 that can be used for functionally grading the finite
sample. Such functional grading was explored in [51] to control the deformation sequence of cells
in a chain, and in [96] to illustrate how it can lead to a wide array of stress-strain responses. Our
optimization-based design approach allows us to directly tailor the grading to maximize energy
dissipated through non-equilibrium transitions and viscoelasticity. In doing so, we show how
the transition between bi-stable and mono-stable behavior can be exploited to drive novel and
non-intuitive responses.
Apex height values are bound between 10 mm and 40 mm. Note that design parameter values given
in C are labeled as ℎ ⟨𝑐⟩ with 𝑐 being the cell in the chain, starting from the bottom. Following
Section 3.2, in the following examples the response is analyzed under displacement control with a
maximum displacement of 350 mm applied over 350 equally-spaced time steps.

 (a) (b)

 Figure 5-5. Results of optimizing a chain of 5 snap-through unit cells using a hyperelastic model:
 (a) force-displacement response, (b) difference between external work and strain energy.

5.2.2.1. Hyperelastic Response

Figure 5-5 shows the optimized response obtained using a hyperelastic model. Again, the hyper-
elastic model was used to focus solely on the energy absorption occurring from non-equilibrium
transitions. The difference between external work and strain energy shown in Figure 5-5b reveals a
stair step response with discrete jumps corresponding to the snap-through of individual unit cells.
In the initial design with uniform nominal apex height values, each consecutive snap-through event
leads to a larger jump as more strain energy has built up in cells that have already snapped. However,
in the optimized design varying the apex heights (see Table C-4) causes the largest jump to occur
during the third snap-through event. This can be observed in Figure 5-6 which shows the strain
energy distribution in the deformed configuration at different points before and after snap-through
events. In the third snap-through event (Figure 5-6 (c) and (d)) the top unit cell undergoing snap-
through has a larger apex height than the ones that have already snapped. This results in a larger
jump in configuration (and thus larger release of strain energy) as the snap-through event occurs.
In fact, this large jump in configuration causes the second unit cell from the bottom to jump back

 69

into the pre-snapped configuration (Figure 5-6 (d)). As a result this unit cell ends up undergoing
snap-through twice, which is observed as an extra peak in the force-displacement curve and an
extra jump in the dissipated energy curve around a displacement of 100mm (Figure 5-5).

It is important to note that the nominal apex heights cause bi-stable behavior in each unit cell (see
Figure 3-1b), which locks them into the configuration obtained after snap-through. However, both
bi-stable and mono-stable behavior is possible within the apex height bounds used during shape
optimization. The optimizer is thus able to determine a combination of apex heights that exploit
the transition between bi-stable and mono-stable responses to drive multiple snap-through events
in a single unit cell undergoing monotonic uniaxial compression. Uncovering such non-intuitive
behavior is only possible by directly modeling the full chain of unit cells with a nonlinear solver
that can robustly handle large jumps in configuration, enabling the energy dissipation to be directly
quantified and thus maximized during shape optimization.

  Figure 5-6. Strain energy density (MPa) distribution in deformed configuration for a chain of 5
snap-through unit cells with optimized apex heights using a hyperelastic model: (a)-(b) before and
  after the first snap-through event, (c)-(d) before and after the third snap-through event, (e)-(f)
 before and after the fifth and final snap-through event.
 70

5.2.2.2. Viscoelastic Response

Section 3.3 discusses how viscoelastic relaxation changes the response of the five cell chain
(Figure 3-3b). Interestingly, at higher Weissenberg numbers the individual cells undergo smaller
configurational changes and snap-back response can be lost. In the next example, we explore
how this change in response affects the design of the five cell chain when considering energy
dissipation. To illustrate this, we use a Weissenberg number 𝑊𝑖 = 1 × 10−2 . At this Weissenberg
number viscoelastic dissipation is non-negligible and is accounted for in the dissipated energy
objective along with the release of strain energy occurring due to non-equilibrium transitions.

 (a) (b)

 Figure 5-7. Results of optimizing a chain of 5 snap-through unit cells using a viscoelastic model
 and Weissenberg number 𝑊𝑖 = 1 × 10−2 : (a) force-displacement response, (b) difference between
 external work and strain energy.

Figure 5-7 compares the nominal response to the response of an optimized design. Note that
this design was not obtained by using the nominal design as the starting point, but instead used
a starting design near the optimized design obtained with the hyperelastic model (apex heights
ℎ1 = 16 mm, ℎ2 = 13 mm, ℎ3 = 13 mm, ℎ4 = 19 mm, ℎ5 = 22 mm). In contrast to the nominal
design, the optimized design has five distinct peaks with sharper drop-offs, bringing it more in-line
with the nominal hyperelastic force-displacement response. The reasons for this can be ascertained
through the history of the dissipated energy as shown in Figure 5-7b. Compared to the hyperelastic
response (Figure 5-5b), the energy dissipation is far more monotonic with a Weissenberg number
𝑊𝑖 = 1 × 10−2 . Indeed, in the nominal design there is no discernible stair-stepping effect but a
significant amount of energy is dissipated, indicating that viscoelastic dissipation dominates. In
the optimized design, the monotonic increase in dissipation caused by viscoelastic relaxation also
dominates, but there are distinct humps resulting from the discrete snap-through events. This can
also be seen by comparing the strain energy distribution at different points before and after snap-
through events in the nominal design (Figure 3-4) to the strain energy distributions in the optimized
design shown in Figure 5-8, where a more marked release of strain energy is observed, especially
in the later snap-through events. Hence, the optimizer is able to grade the apex heights in such a
way that larger jumps in configuration are undergone by the individual cells. This dissipates more
energy overall by increasing the contributions from non-equilibrium transitions while maintaining
the dominant viscoelastic energy dissipation mechanisms.

 71

  Figure 5-8. Strain energy density (MPa) distribution in deformed configuration for a chain of 5
 snap-through unit cells with optimized apex heights using a Weissenberg number 𝑊𝑖 = 1 × 10−2 :
(a)-(b) before and after the first snap-through event, (c)-(d) before and after the third snap-through
 event, (e)-(f) before and after the fifth and final snap-through event.

 72

5.3. Design of Buckling Metamaterials

In this section we demonstrate how our optimization-based design framework can also be applied to
tailor the response of soft metamaterials that exploit buckling instabilities to undergo programmable
pattern transformations. The effect of pore shape on the nonlinear response of the buckling
metamaterial studied in Section 3.4 has been studied extensively [230, 207], including in a recent
work that performed shape optimization to tailor pore shape for targeted nonlinear responses [187].
We thus use this system to highlight unique aspects of our approach, namely how the robustness
of the trust region solver enables navigation of an energy landscape that changes based on the
geometry [40] and the rate of deformation.
Each of the 25 pores is realized as a superellipse, defined by the equation

 𝑥 𝑛 𝑦𝑛
 + =1 (5.1)
 𝑎 𝑏
where we set 𝑎 = 𝑏 to keep the same semi-diameter in both dimensions. The curvature of the
superellipse is controlled by the exponent 𝑛. This allows us to vary the pore shape from diamond-
like (𝑛 = 1.5) to square-like (𝑛 = 4) with a value 𝑛 = 2 corresponding to a circle. The set of design
parameters 𝝓 for this example thus contains 25 design parameters each corresponding to a value of
𝑛 bounded between 1.5 and 4. As the area of the superellipse is not uniform for every value of 𝑛,
each pore is scaled so as to have the same area as a circle (𝑛 = 2), resulting in a constant porosity
near 60 %. All design examples in this section use a starting design with circular pores, i.e. all 25
design parameters are initialized with a value 𝑛 = 2. Note that design parameter values given in
C are labeled as 𝑛 ⟨𝑟⟩⟨𝑐⟩ with 𝑟 being the row and 𝑐 the column, starting from the bottom left. The
boundary conditions illustrated in Figure 3-5a and the trust region settings given in Section 3.4 are
utilized for all examples. Finally, we report the optimized design parameter values for each of the
examples in Appendix C.

5.3.1. Example 1: Designing for Mechanical Fuse Behavior

This first example highlights how our approach can be used to achieve a desired nonlinear response
by exploiting instabilities in the presence of viscoelastic effects. As shown in Section 3.4, soft
metamaterials exhibiting buckling instabilities may experience suppression of such instabilities at
high enough strain rates. This could be problematic for applications where such a metamaterial is
employed as a mechanical fuse. In this scenario, the stress plateau occurring after the critical point
is needed to prevent the transfer of force to a payload. Failing to properly account for the interplay
between instabilities and viscoelastic relaxation can instead lead to designs that do not meet the
proper requirements.
The hyperelastic response and viscoelastic response with a Weissenberg number 𝑊𝑖 = 1 × 10−2
of the initial geometry with circular pores under uniaxial compression with a 5 mm downward
displacement applied over 40 equally-spaced time steps is shown in Figure 5-9b. Due to viscoelastic
relaxation the buckling response becomes suppressed and the mechanical fuse behavior is lost,
resulting in an unacceptably large force transmitted to a payload. This can be observed in Figure

 73

 (a) (b)

 Figure 5-9. Results of buckling metamaterial optimization to obtain a mechanical fuse: (a)
optimization convergence, (b) force-displacement responses. Viscoelastic responses are obtained
 using a Weissenberg number 𝑊𝑖 = 1 × 10−2 .

5-10, where the left column shows the deformed configuration of this initial geometry. While some
of the pores are deforming, the structure hasn’t reached the polarized configuration occurring after
the bifurcation point (see for reference Figure 3-5a).

To recover the mechanical fuse behavior, we first seek to design the metamaterial so that its force-
displacement response at a Weissenberg number 𝑊𝑖 = 1×10−2 reproduces the observed hyperelastic
behavior. The L2 difference norm objective Eq. 4.1 is used with four equally spaced target points,
shown with a red X in Figure 5-9b and referred to as target 1. Figure 5-9b also shows the response
obtained with the optimized geometry (the Viscoelastic optimized 1 curve). It is clear that the
optimizer struggles to match the target points, which indicates a lack of sufficient flexibility in the
geometric parameterization.

As verification, we use the total work objective Eq. 4.2 and minimize it to obtain the softest possible
response for this geometry parameterization. The force-displacement response for this case is also
plotted in Figure 5-9b, where it can be seen to coincide almost perfectly with the response obtained
using the L2 difference norm for target 1. Additionally, a comparison of the optimized design
parameter values in Table C-5 shows that similar designs are produced for the design obtained with
the L2 difference norm with target 1 and the design obtained by minimizing the total work. This
indicates that the viscoelastic response at a Weissenberg number 𝑊𝑖 = 1 × 10−2 cannot be softened
further and the lowest peak force obtainable is around 3.25 N.

With this in mind, we aim to design the metamaterial to perform as a mechanical fuse at the lowest
possible force value for the given geometric parameterization. That is, we want to design the
metamaterial to buckle at the lowest possible peak force (3.25 N) and maintain a plateau thereafter.
The target points are shown in Figure 5-9b along with the force-displacement response of the
optimized geometry (target 2). The optimized geometry can be seen to match the target points quite
well. The lack of softening in this geometry compared to the optimized geometry obtained for
target 1 can be explained by comparing the geometries and deformed configurations in Figure 5-10
(center and right columns). By utilizing pores that are more square-like, the geometry for target 2

 74

 Figure 5-10. Buckling metamaterial geometries and deformed configurations at the maximum
applied displacement of 5 mm using a Weissenberg number 𝑊𝑖 = 1 × 10−2 . The left column contains
the initial geometry, center column the optimized geometry for target 1, right column the optimized
 geometry for target 2.

 75

contains thicker ligaments towards the interior of the metamaterial that stiffen the post-bifurcation
response.

5.3.2. Example 2: Maximizing Viscoelastic Energy Dissipation

 (a) (b)

 Figure 5-11. Force-displacement response of buckling metamaterial designs optimized to tailor
 viscoelastic energy dissipation: (a) Weissenberg number 𝑊𝑖 = 1 × 10−2 , (b) Weissenberg number
                                           𝑊𝑖 = 1 × 10−3 .

We next study how our optimization-based design approach can be used to tailor viscoelastic
energy dissipation in soft metamaterials undergoing buckling instabilities. This again demonstrates
how viscoelasticity can be harnessed to drive desired behavior, but also highlights the ability of
our approach to drive changes in the buckling response without explicitly prescribing a desired
force-displacement curve. This is in contrast to the example explored in Section 5.3.1 and to other
works in which optimization-based design is used to exploit instabilities [171, 187]. A single
loading-unloading cycle is considered with a maximum applied downward displacement of 5 mm
(10 % strain). The whole loading-unloading cycle is carried out over 80 equally-spaced time steps.
Under this scenario, any external work being done at the end of the cycle is mostly dissipated, as
bringing the applied displacement back to 0 during unloading ensures the strain energy has been
mostly released. Hence, we can approximate the total dissipation as the total external work done
on the system at the end of the loading cycle. With this in mind, we maximize (1) the total external
work done at the end of the cycle (Eq. 4.2) and (2) the dissipation efficiency. Here, we define
dissipation efficiency as the ratio of the energy dissipated over the entire cycle (Eq. 4.4) to the
external work applied during the loading phase (Eq. 4.2 with 𝑛 corresponding to the last time step
during the loading portion).

Figure 5-11a shows the response of optimized designs using a Weissenberg number 𝑊𝑖 = 1 × 10−2 .
Here it can be seen that the different objectives lead to designs which may suppress the buckling
instability or exploit it. Maximizing the total work leads to the stiffest possible response, where all
pores are square-like (superellipse exponent n = 4) (see Figure 5-12 and Table C-6). By keeping
the buckling instability suppressed and stiffening the response, this design requires more work to

 76

Figure 5-12. Buckling metamaterial geometries and dissipated energy density (MPa) distributions
 in deformed configuration at the maximum applied displacement of 5 mm using a Weissenberg
number 𝑊𝑖 = 1 × 10−2 . The left column contains the initial geometry, center column the geometry
 obtained by maximizing total work, and right column the geometry obtained by maximizing the
 dissipation efficiency.

 77

be done on the metamaterial in order to reach the applied displacement. As a result, the highest
overall amount of energy is dissipated by this design (Table 5-1).

 Table 5-1. Energy dissipation performance of initial and optimized designs using a Weissenberg
                                      number 𝑊𝑖 = 1 × 10−2
 Design Dissipated Energy Dissipation Efficiency
         Initial               15.457 mJ               0.757 %
      Total Work               17.048 mJ               0.755 %
 Dissipation Efficiency 13.492 mJ 0.820 %

In contrast, maximizing the dissipation efficiency leads to a design which harnesses a buckling
instability. This causes a softer response, requiring less work to reach the maximum applied
displacement and resulting in less overall energy being dissipated. However, this design dissipates
more energy per unit of work done during loading than the other designs, as illustrated in Table 5-1.
The mechanism driving this behavior is revealed by visualizing the dissipated energy density in
the deformed configuration, i.e. the right column of Figure 5-12. There, a higher concentration of
dissipated energy is observed in comparison to the other designs. This dissipation is concentrated in
the ligaments which experience bending as the geometry undergoes a large configurational change
due to the buckling instability. Hence, harnessing a buckling instability allows for a larger portion of
the energy put into deforming this metamaterial to be dissipated due to viscoelastic effects. While
advantageous for energy dissipation, such highly concentrated deformations could lead to localized
failure. Designs that achieve high energy dissipation by spreading the viscoelastic deformation
throughout the structure rather than concentrating it in a few locations can be encouraged through
the inclusion of constraints on local measures such as stress [171] or strain energy [76]. Such an
approach has been demonstrated with topology optimization for plastic energy dissipation in, e.g.,
[167, 5, 168], and we leave its incorporation into the current framework as a subject for future
research.

Next, we carry out the same optimization study but with a Weissenberg number 𝑊𝑖 = 1 × 10−3 . In
this regime, the initial geometry’s buckling instability is not suppressed and viscoelastic dissipation
is lower overall due to the lower rate. The results are shown in Figure 5-11b, where it can be seen
that both optimized designs have a significantly higher critical force. Interestingly, these designs
both consist of relatively small perturbations around the initial circular pore shape, as shown in
Figure 5-13 and Table C-7, but these small perturbations cause a marked difference in the deformed
configuration (Figure 5-13). Here, it can be seen that the deformed pores have a polarization
that is opposite of the initial design. Both optimized designs have the center pore polarized
vertically, while the initial design has the center pore polarized horizontally. This indicates that
our optimization-based design approach is able to navigate a complex bifurcation landscape to find
geometric perturbations that lead to a more optimal bifurcated solution branch. As can be seen from
Table 5-2, this branch increases both the overall energy dissipated and the dissipation efficiency.
While interesting from an algorithmic perspective, such a design could be difficult to manufacture
due to the level of precision needed to induce the correct bifurcated behavior. In general, the
consideration of manufacturing imperfections is an important topic for these structures, but out of
scope for the current study.

number 𝑊𝑖 = 1 × 10−3 . The left column contains the initial geometry, center column the geometry
 obtained by maximizing total work, and right column the geometry obtained by maximizing the

5.3.3. Example 3: Tailoring the Response Using Different Viscoelastic Models

As a final example, we demonstrate that our approach can be used to tailor the response of
metamaterials with more complex viscoelastic behavior corresponding to a rubber-like PolyJet
Photopolymer. To do so, we utilize three Maxwell branches with relaxation times and non-
equilibrium shear moduli corresponding to the three-branch model calibrated for Stratasys Agilus
 𝑛𝑒𝑞 𝑛𝑒𝑞 𝑛𝑒𝑞
30 in [87]. These are 𝐺 1 = 1.161 MPa, 𝐺 2 = 0.671 MPa, 𝐺 3 = 0.258 MPa, 𝜏1 = 0.047
s, 𝜏2 = 0.97 s, 𝜏3 = 18.0 s. For the equilibrium branch, 𝐺 = 0.860 MPa and 𝜅 = 860 MPa.
The response of the three-branch model is compared to the response of a single-branch model
with properties also calibrated for Stratasys Agilus 30 in [87]. These are 𝐺 𝑛𝑒𝑞 = 1.832 MPa and
𝜏 = 0.43 s. We utilize the L2 difference norm objective Eq. 4.1 with four equally-spaced target
points, chosen to encourage mechanical fuse behavior as in Section 5.3.1.

 (a) (b)

 Figure 5-14. Results of buckling metamaterial optimization to obtain a target force-displacement
 response using different viscoelastic models at a rate corresponding to 𝑊𝑖 = 1 × 10−2 for the
 single-branch model: (a) force-displacement responses, (b) geometries and deformed

 and the right column containing the optimized geometry for the single-branch model.

Figure 5-14 shows the results obtained using a rate corresponding to a Weissenberg number
𝑊𝑖 = 1 × 10−2 with the relaxation time 𝜏 = 0.43 s for the single-branch model. It can be seen
that the initial responses for the single-branch and three-branch models are quite different, but
the optimized responses obtained with both models match the target response quite closely. The
optimized geometries in Figure 5-14b show that our framework is able to tailor pore shapes to
exploit the different underlying viscoelastic response of each material model and obtain the same
overall buckling-driven force-displacement response (see also Table C-8). This example illustrates
that our approach is not limited to simplistic viscoelastic models, but can be used to tailor the
response of soft metamaterials with more complex viscoelastic behavior calibrated to 3D printed
elastomers.

 80

5.4. Conclusions

In this chapter, we presented an optimization-based computational design framework for tailoring
the response of soft metamaterials exploiting geometric instabilities in the presence of viscoelastic
relaxation. The use of a minimization approach to obtain stable equilibrium paths in the forward
problem provided the robustness necessary to carry out multiple nonlinear solves over changing
geometric domains within an optimization-based design framework. It also allowed for new
problem formulations to take advantage of energy dissipation occurring during non-equilibrium
transitions. CAD-parameterized shape optimization of metamaterial samples consisting of common
structural motifs provided a number of examples demonstrating the effectiveness of our approach.
In particular, it was shown that shape optimization can be used to exploit snap-through instabilities
for maximizing energy absorption through non-equilibrium transitions and buckling instabilities
for maximizing viscoelastic relaxation. Moreover, by accounting for the complex interplay between
geometric instabilities and rate-dependent material dissipation, unique rate-dependent instability
phenomena were shown to emerge.
This study shows that highly nonlinear phenomena can be incorporated into a gradient-based opti-
mization approach to computational design if care is taken with respect to design parameterizations,
robust nonlinear solvers, and problem formulations. In contrast to machine learning approaches
which must be retrained for different geometric domains and loading conditions, the optimization-
based design approach can be directly applied to different problem types and domains as illustrated
by the array of examples considered in this study. However, the non-convex nature of such design
problems may necessitate the use of multiple initial guesses. Focusing more closely on mitigating
this through different optimization algorithms and settings is left to future studies. It would also be
interesting to pursue a hybrid approach combining some of the strengths of machine learning with
strengths of gradient-based optimization for navigating large and non-convex design spaces.
Future work will focus on incorporating remeshing-based approaches to level-set topology opti-
mization into the framework. It remains to be seen whether this will ameliorate some of the strong
reliance on initial guess that was exhibited in certain CAD-parameterized shape optimization exam-
ples. Consideration of self-contact within the optimization-based design framework is also being
investigated. Another interesting avenue to pursue would include incorporating viscoelastic ef-
fects into homogenized models that can account for the bifurcation-induced microstructural pattern
transformations [251, 271, 239]. This would allow for optimization-based design of structural-scale
geometries made up of these metamaterials. Finally, extending our framework to design energy
absorbing structures under shock loadings [178] is another interesting avenue for future research.

 81

 82

6. ON THE USE OF A CONFORMAL DECOMPOSITION
 METHOD FOR LEVEL-SET TOPOLOGY OPTIMIZATION WITH
 NONLINEAR MECHANICS

Often, the distinction between density-based and level-set approaches in topology optimization
comes down to whether a well-defined representation of structural interfaces is needed. The
density-based parameterization relaxes the problem of finding a discrete characteristic function
representing the presence (1) or absence (0) of material at every point in the design domain to that
of finding a continuous density-like field. By representing this field on a fixed background mesh
which is also used for evaluation of the physics problem [232], a blurred interface is introduced.
The level-set approach instead parameterizes the design as an implicit level-set field defined on
the fixed background mesh and uses the iso-contours this field to represent the structural interface
[252]. While this provides a well-defined implicit interface, some method of mapping it to a
structural domain is required for evaluating the physics problem. This was originally done by
keeping the interface implicit and using the level-set field to define a density distribution with
a blurred interface [263, 13]. An alternative is to retain an implicit interface but use immersed
boundary techniques to represent a sharp boundary in solution spaces used to solve the physics
problem. The extended finite element method (XFEM) – wherein additional shape functions are
utilized to enrich the solution field near the interface – is most commonly used in these approaches
[30, 157, 229]. A third approach is to obtain an explicit representation of the structural interface
by generating a conforming – or body-fitted – discretization of the zero iso-contour [117, 10, 11].
A well-defined structural interface is often desired for problems where the physics at the interface
must be resolved, e.g. fluid-structure interaction [142, 93, 234] and contact [162, 163] problems.
Another reason for desiring a well-defined interface is to avoid the need to represent, interpolate,
and penalize the physical response of an ersatz material.
In density-based approaches and level-set approaches that define a density distribution the fictitious,
or ersatz, material provides an interpolated continuous response between the presence and absence
of material. This approach is efficient and simple to implement, but introduces non-physical
intermediate density values. To reduce the presence of intermediate density values, penalization
techniques such as the ubiquitous SIMP approach [223, 32] are used to degrade the optimality
of material within regions of intermediate densities. In combination with projection techniques
[114, 260], SIMP-based methods have proven highly flexible and effective. However, when more
complicated physics and material responses need to be modeled, the construction of appropriate
interpolation, penalization, and projection strategies for ersatz material becomes burdensome.
This is especially evident in the case of nonlinear solid mechanics, where large deformations and
nonlinear constitutive models require specialized techniques.
Drawbacks of the ersatz material approach for use in nonlinear mechanics were identified in
early works on the subject [184, 49]. These can be roughly divided into two main issues: (i)

 83

excessive mesh distortions in low- and intermediate-density elements which hamper convergence
of the nonlinear solver, and (ii) issues in determining effective interpolation and penalization
rules for more complex, i.e. inelastic, constitutive responses. With regard to the former, a
number of strategies such as modification of the internal force vector [49], element removal and
reintroduction [48], use of artificial springs [278], and relaxation based on a measure of element
distortion [161] were proposed before the community largely settled on an approach that interpolates
between geometrically nonlinear and linear strain energy [261]. When combined with suitable
projection methods, this approach is effective at mitigating the influence of excessive distortions
in low- and intermediate-density elements [55, 281, 170, 70]. The latter issue – devising effective
interpolation and penalization rules for inelastic constitutive models – has also received much
attention in the literature, but remains a problem-dependent exercise. Different approaches have
been proposed for e.g. viscoelasticity [138, 280], plasticity [184, 36, 148], and continuum damage
models [137, 167]. Each approach requires careful consideration of how material constants in the
constitutive model affect the optimality of the constitutive response for a given quantity of interest.
Nevertheless, a number of studies have incorporated inelastic constitutive models in density-based
[258, 19, 5, 135, 168, 6, 282, 136, 153, 283, 37, 134, 2, 287, 172, 112, 143, 144, 8] and ersatz
material level-set [183, 212, 122, 191] approaches.

Both of the aforementioned issues with the ersatz material approach can be avoided outright
by employing conforming meshes during topology optimization. This was recognized in early
work with large deformation elasticity [117], where a Delaunay triangulation scheme was used
to generate conforming meshes at each optimization iteration. It was also recognized early on
for small strain plasticity in [184], where a staggered approach was carried out in cycles wherein
density-based topology optimization was first performed, the resulting iso-surface used to generate
a spline parameterization for shape optimization, and a conforming mesh generated for use in the
next cycle. Since these early works, a number of approaches that utilize conforming meshes have
been proposed, although the vast majority do not focus on nonlinear mechanics. A robust approach
to obtaining a conforming mesh at each iteration was presented in [10, 11, 12], where a simplicial
mesh is first generated from the level-set iso-contour and then modified locally for improved
quality and geometric approximation. This approach was further developed and applied to fluid-
structure [93] and fluid-fluid [94] thermal systems, and small strain plasticity [78]. Around the
same time, a method employing a deformable simplicial complex (DSC) that explicitly conforms
to the structural interface and can be evolved by optimizing the position of nodes using shape
sensitivities was proposed in [62]. This method was later extended to consider stress constraints
[174], and incorporation with density-based methods and mesh coarsening [199]. In [196], trimmed
hexahedral meshes (with polyhedral elements) that conform the the level-set iso-contour were
generated with a marching cubes algorithm. The extension of this approach in the context of stress-
constrained problems was demonstrated in [197, 198, 125]. A number of more recent works have
employed different approaches for generating conforming meshes in level-set topology optimization,
see e.g. [159, 291, 227, 290, 226], although none have focused on nonlinear mechanics.

In this chapter, we show that a level-set topology optimization approach employing conforming
meshes enables more robust solution of topology optimization problems involving nonlinear me-
chanics. We utilize a conformal decomposition approach [156] similar to that in [10, 11, 12]. In
contrast to practically all existing level-set approaches employing conforming meshes, we evolve

 84

the level-set field explicitly using nonlinear programming techniques as opposed to solving the
Hamilton-Jacobi equation.

6.1. Conformal Decomposition Level-Set Topology Optimization
