 
 

 85

The two terms in the regularization penalty 𝑓 𝑃 serve two different functions. The first term
encourages the level-set field 𝑝ℎ𝑖  ˜ to be close to 𝜙ˆ away from the interface. This helps to remove
spurious oscillations of material far away from the interface where sensitivities are defined. The
second term encourages the gradient of the level-set field ∇ 𝜙˜ to resemble the gradient of a signed-
distance field ∇ 𝜙ˆ close to the interface. The uniform and unit norm properties of the signed-distance
field gradient encourage smooth evolution of the level-set interface.

6.1.2. Conformal Decomposition Finite Element Method (CDFEM)

As described in Section 4.1, we desire a geometry module that can map a set of design parameters
𝝓 to a conformal finite element mesh of the geometric domain Ω𝑎 ( 𝑿) defined by nodal coordinates
𝑿 (𝝓). The geometry module employed for level-set topology optimization must thus generate a
conformal finite element mesh based on the level-set field 𝜙. ˜ To do so, we employ a Conformal
Decomposition Finite Element Method (CDFEM) approach. As described in [156], CDFEM is an
approach for the sharp representation of a moving interface in a finite element (FE) mesh. The
 ˜ As the level-set field is a diffuse representation
interface location is described by a level-set field 𝜙.
of the interface, elements on the background mesh may contain nodes with both positive and
negative level-set values. CDFEM seeks to convert this diffuse interface representation to a sharp
representation by modifying the mesh.

First, intersections with the background mesh edges and the level-set = 0 isocontour are identified.
This is trivially accomplished by finding all edges whose nodes have dissimilar level-set field
signs and then interpolating along the edge using the FE basis functions to the zero level-set value
location. In the basic CDFEM implementation, each intersection point that does not coincide with
an existing mesh node is inserted into the mesh as a new node. Edges are then created to connect
new nodes to preexisting mesh nodes to form new elements. The net effect is that each element
that spans the level-set = 0 isocontour is sub-divided into child elements whose nodes lie on the
interface. This method is referred to as cutting and results in a new, conformal mesh Ω𝑎 ( 𝑿) in
which the interface described by the level-set field is now represented explicitly.

Depending on how close the level-set edge intersections are to existing nodes, elements with
very high aspect ratios and thus poor quality can be created. In order to address this, an option
exists in CDFEM to perform a preliminary snapping operation prior to cutting. During snapping,
intersection points are identified where cutting will likely lead to poor element quality. At these
locations, existing mesh nodes are moved along their intersected edge to the level-set = 0 position.
Since the moving of nodes impacts the level-set intersection calculation in other elements, snapping
is done iteratively, with sets of independent snapping operations performed until no more are
identified. Once all snaps have been performed, cutting proceeds as described previously. The
snapping method also results in an explicit representation of the level-set interface on a conformal
mesh Ω𝑎 ( 𝑿), but with improved mesh quality. This is particularly important for applications
sensitive to quality, such as solid mechanics.

 86

6.1.3. CDFEM Sensitivity Calculations

The geometry module must also provide the derivative of the mapping from 𝝓 to Ω𝑎 ( 𝑿), i.e.
𝑑 𝑿 𝑑𝝓. This derivative is obtained by computing sensitiviy values of the interface node locations
to the design variables 𝝓. In this work, we restrict ourselves to linear triangular and tetrahedral
mesh elements, for which the sensitivity calculations become quite simple. All interface node
locations on the conformal mesh lie on an edge between two parent nodes on the background mesh.
For linear elements, this means that each interface node location is a linear combination of the
locations of its parent nodes:

                                        𝑿 = (1 − 𝛼) 𝑿1 + 𝛼𝑿2                                      (6.5)

The blending coefficient, 𝛼, is dependent on the level-set values at the parent nodes through the
solution for the zero level-set location along the edge:

                                         (1 − 𝛼)𝑙1 + 𝛼𝑙 2 = 0                                     (6.6)

Solving Equation 6.6 for 𝛼, plugging into Equation 6.5, and differentiating with respect to 𝑙1 and 𝑙2
gives the sensitivities:

 𝜕𝑿 −𝑙2
 = ( 𝑿2 − 𝑿1 )
                                     𝜕𝑙 1 (𝑙2 − 𝑙 1 ) 2
 (6.7)
 𝜕𝑿 𝑙1
 = ( 𝑿2 − 𝑿1 )
                                     𝜕𝑙 2 (𝑙2 − 𝑙 1 ) 2

In the case of cut nodes, computing the sensitivities is straightforward as mesh data structures
allow the cut nodes to be traced back to their parent edge. For snapped nodes, the process is more
complex. A displacement field is added to the mesh that tracks the difference between the original
and snapped locations. Thus, for any node snapped to the interface, the original location can be
recovered. All edges containing the node are then iterated to find the one that contains the snapped
location. Sensitivities can then be calculated as for cut nodes.

Nodes on the background mesh where the level-set field is already 0 (a background mesh node that
is an interface node) are the most problematic. In this case, it is not known which direction the node
will move in response to small changes in the level-set field values, as it can potentially move along
any of several connected edges. Level-set field changes could also result in the single background
mesh node being replaced with multiple interface cut nodes. In order to provide a sensitivity value
in this situation, an edge to travel along is arbitrarily chosen by finding the connected node with the
largest difference in level-set field value. However, it is understood that this case is a discontinuity
and the derivatives are not well defined.

 87

6.2. Numerical Examples

We now demonstrate our approach to level-set topology optimization with some illustrative ex-
amples. For all optimization problems in this section we aim to maximize the stiffness under
prescribed displacements. To do so, we choose as our objective the total work, Eq. 4.2. We
include the penalty term Eq. 6.3 in our objective in order to regularize the evolution of the level-set
field. As we aim to maximize the total work 𝑓𝑤𝑜𝑟 𝑘 and minimize the regularization penalty 𝑓 𝑃 , we
formulate the total objective as follows

 𝑓0
                                         𝑓 = 𝑤 0 𝑤𝑜𝑟 𝑘 + 𝑤 1 𝑓 𝑃                                 (6.8)
 𝑓𝑤𝑜𝑟 𝑘
where 𝑤 0 and 𝑤 1 are weights for the different terms. By minimizing 𝑓 we will encourage maxi-
mization of the total work 𝑓𝑤𝑜𝑟 𝑘 while minimizing the regularization penalty 𝑓 𝑃 . We also normalize
 0
the total work term by scaling by the total work at the initial optimization iteration, 𝑓𝑤𝑜𝑟  𝑘 . This
allows for the weights 𝑤 0 and 𝑤 1 to be kept constant for different optimization problems. These
weights are set to 𝑤 0 = 1.0 and 𝑤 1 = 0.1 for all examples.
We also constrain the volume of the design, as computed over the conforming mesh Ω𝑎 ( 𝑿), to be
below a prescribed value. The constraint has the form
 ∫ ∫
                                𝑔(𝝓) =                 𝑑𝑉 − 𝑉 𝑓         𝑑𝑉 ≤ 0                   (6.9)
 Ω𝑎 ( 𝑿 (𝝓)) Ωℎ𝐷

where 𝑉 𝑓 is the prescribed fraction of the background mesh Ωℎ𝐷 volume. As the mapping between
design parameters 𝝓 and nodes 𝑿 on the conformal mesh Ω𝑎 ( 𝑿) is nonlinear, the constraint 𝑔(𝝓)
is nonlinear. To carry out optimization we use the sequential quadratic programming algorithm
described in Section 4.5.

6.2.1. Cantilever Beam

 (a) (b)

  Figure 6-1. Cantilever beam problem: (a) background mesh and boundary conditions, (b) initial
 level-set field.

We first consider a cantilever beam domain to demonstrate how our approach captures the influence
of large deformations on the optimized designs. The problem was first studied in [49, 47] and
illustrates the distinct design features that occur when large deformations are included. The
rectangular design domain has dimensions of 1.0 m by 0.25 m and is shown in Figure 6-1b

 88

along with the boundary conditions. The left-hand side of the domain is fixed and a downward
displacement is applied over three nodes at the center of the right-hand side using 40 equally-spaced
time steps. For this example we employ the Neo-Hookean model with the form shown in Eq. 2.12.
with Young’s modulus 𝐸 = 3.0 GPa and Poisson’s ratio 𝜈 = 0.4. The initial level-set field used in
all optimization runs is shown in Figure 6-1b.

 (a)

 (b)

 (c)

  Figure 6-2. Optimized topologies obtained for cantilever example: (a) 𝑢¯ 𝑛 = 0.25, (b) 𝑢¯ 𝑛 = 0.5, (c)
 𝑢¯ 𝑛 = 1.0.

Three different values of the applied displacement 𝑢¯ 𝑛 are utilized and the resulting optimized
topologies are shown in Figure 6-2. A few observations can be made. First, while vertical symmetry
would be expected if the material was linear elastic, all three designs are asymmetric. This is a
hallmark of geometrically nonlinear designs, as pointed out in [49, 47]. Second, as the applied
displacement is increased the bar on the right-hand side that supports the applied displacement
increases in length. Again, this is an expected result in geometrically nonlinear designs [49, 47].
At the largest applied displacement, this feature is exaggerated greatly, a result that was obtained
previously [261, 257].

 89

 (a) (b) (c)

 Figure 6-3. Deformed configurations at the three levels of applied displacement: (a) design
  obtained using 𝑢¯ 𝑛 = 0.25, (b) design obtained using 𝑢¯ 𝑛 = 0.5, (c) design obrained using 𝑢¯ 𝑛 = 1.0.

The reason for the exaggerated length of the right-most bar is revealed by observing the deformed
configurations for each of the three designs at the different levels of applied displacement. This is
shown in Figure 6-3. Here, it can be seen that at the lowest applied displacement, designs with an
elongated bar have a relatively soft response, as this bar simply rotates as the displacement is applied.
As the displacement is increased, however, this bar gets stretched directly downward, increasing
the stiffness of the structure. Indeed, as the displacement is increased to 𝑢¯ 𝑛 = 0.5 the design
obtained using 𝑢¯ 𝑛 = 0.25 experiences significant buckling. The same occurs to the design obtained
using 𝑢¯ 𝑛 = 0.5 when the displacement is increased to 𝑢¯ 𝑛 = 1.0. These results demonstrate that
the optimized topologies are tailored specifically to their level of applied displacement. While this
can result in a degenerate design that becomes sub-optimal under different applied displacement
levels, it does demonstrate that the large deformation response is correctly captured during the
optimization process.

 90

7. MATERIAL LAYOUT OPTIMIZATION FOR CONTROLLED
 MECHANICAL PROPERTIES IN SOFT METAMATERIALS VIA
 GRAYSCALE DIGITAL LIGHT PROCESSING

An emerging trend in additive manufacturing technologies is to incorporate two or more materials
during the printing process to generate multi-material 3D objects with spatially varying physical
properties such as mechanical stiffness and strength or thermal conductivity [146]. Multi-material
3D printers often utilize specialized tool heads and material exchanging systems that are capable of
readily switching between materials to control the selective deposition of material within a single
layer [289]. For example, functionally graded materials have been fabricated using a multi-material
direct ink writing (DIW) method that switches printing heads during a single layer to deposit up to
four different materials for thermal management devices with spatially varying thermal conductivity
[235]. Functionally graded metals have also been recently demonstrated with spatial varying
physical properties such as corrosion resistance and thermal conductivity [216]. Additionally,
digital light processing (DLP) 3D printing has been used to fabricate a multi-material lattice by
switching material within the vat through various inlets, using a neutral and negatively charged
polymer for electro-less deposition that can realize metal and polymer multi-material printed parts
[265]. However, printing multi-material structures is generally slow due to the material exchange
process that occurs multiple times within a single layer, and can potentially result in undesired
mixing. Recent research advances in vat photopolymerization 3D printing approaches have shown
that modulating the grayscale intensity changes the degree of cross-linking within the polymer
matrix, allowing printed parts to be endowed with different mechanical properties [158]. This
grayscale DLP has been used to produce elastomer 3D objects with large mechanical property
differences (e.g. Young’s modulus ranging from 0.016 MPa to 478 MPa) printed using single vat
technology without material switching [279]. Moreover, the grayscale intensity can be adjusted
on the individual voxel level, enabling a high degree of control over how material properties are
laid out, as demonstrated for bioinspired soft robots such as pneumatic actuators with tailorable
deformed shape responses in [285]. Most existing works resort to heuristic grading approaches,
build-and-test cycles, or simple one-way coupling with simulation results to generate grayscale
designs [158, 279, 285], but these approaches are not suited to exploit the extremely large design
space that is unlocked by the high degree of control that grayscale DLP provides.

In this chapter, we utilize an optimization-based approach to exploit the large design space afforded
by grayscale DLP for tailoring the nonlinear and linearized response of soft metamaterials. To
do so, we optimize the local distribution of design variables within a fixed geometry to obtain
light intensity maps that produce spatially varied mechanical stiffness distributions when printed
with grayscale DLP. We demonstrate the utility of this approach by designing, printing, and testing
specimens that highlight unique buckling and self-contact responses that derive from the optimized
spatial distribution of different mechanical properties.

 91

7.1. Grayscale Digital Light Processing

7.1.1. Printing Approach

High resolution grayscale DLP was performed by depositing a photocurable resin within a resin vat
where ultraviolet (UV) light is projected onto the resin tank to promote free radical polymerization.
Grayscale images, representing a small layer of the cross-sectional area in the digital model,
were projected through a digital micromirror device (DMD) onto the bottom of the resin vat to
selectively solidify the photocurable material. After the previous layer has been cured, the build
platform ascends to allow fresh material to be deposited between the printed structure and the
bottom of the resin vat. This process was repeated in a layer-by-layer manner to construct the entire
digital model. Light intensity was adjusted between the full intensity grayscale (G = 255) and low
intensity grayscale (G = 51) to control the cross-linking density of the photocurable material. After
3D printing, the printed parts were ultrasonicated in an ethanol bath for 30 minutes before being
placed in an oven at T = 120 o C for 12 hours to dry the residual solvent and thermally cure the
polymer network. The printer settings were optimized to fabricate soft metamaterial lattices and
tensile specimens with high resolution and accuracy.

The photosensitive printing ink was prepared by first adding bisphenol A ethoxylate diacrylate,
glycidyl methacrylate, and n-butyl acrylate with a mass ratio equivalent to 69:23:8 into a UV
resistant amber bottle and stirred until thoroughly mixed. Then, the photoinitiator phenylbis
(2,4,6-trimethylbenzoyl) phosphine oxide (Igracure 819) and photo-absorber Sudan I were added
to the acrylate-based solution in weight percentages of 0.7% (w/w) and 0.07% (w/w), respectively.
Finally, poly(propylene glycol) bis(aminopropyl ether) or D230 was added to the mixture using a
weight percentage of 7% (w/w) to obtain the photosensitive 3D printing resin.

The degree of curing (DoC) or cross-linking density within the resin is directly related to the light
intensity and exposure time using higher applied energies, resulting in a larger DoC when compared
to lower applied energies. As shown in Figure 7-1a, the measured light intensity linearly increases
from 4 to 18 mW/cm2 by changing the grayscale value from G51 to G255. This increasing light
intensity can be utilized to generate non-uniform DoC within a single layer allowing for a high
degree of control over the mechanical properties throughout the entire structure. To demonstrate,
microfilms with a height of 1 mm were fabricated with an alternating grayscale gradient (G23
to G255) to determine the relationship between curing height, a direct observation of the overall
polymerization, and the resolution of the DLP 3D printer. The alternating gradient pattern tested
decreased from 1000 micron gap spacing to 100 micron gap spacing with a pixel side length of
20 and 2 pixels, respectively (see Figure 7-1b). At the lowest gap spacing (100 micron) a clear
distinction can be made between the edge of the microfilm where the gradient has the sharpest
transition between grayscale values indicating different photo-polymerization kinetics. Moreover,
within the middle region of the microfilm the curing height is relatively constant showing a more
uniform distribution of cross-linking density. This demonstrates that microscale resolution can
be achieved to control the mechanical properties on the individual pixel scale. The energy dose
was adjusted for the DLP-based printer to optimize the resolution of soft metamaterial lattices to
mitigate over-curing and formation of internal pores.

 92

  Figure 7-1. Light intensity and mechanical property relationship showing (a) light intensity as a
 function of grayscale percentage; (b) gradient grayscale films with varying side lengths; (c)
stress-strain curves of varying grayscale values; and (d) elastic modulus and grayscale percentage
 relationship.

7.1.2. Mechanical Properties

To determine the mechanical properties at different grayscale values, tensile samples with grayscale
values ranging from full light intensity (G255) to 20% light intensity (G51) were fabricated using
the printing process described above. The samples were loaded onto a universal testing machine
(Mark-10, Instron Instrument) with a 1500 N load cell at a displacement rate of 1 mm/min. Stress-
strain properties were evaluated using ASTM standard 661 with a minimum of 4 tensile specimens
for each tested grayscale value.

As depicted in Figure 7-1c, yielding begins for each grayscale value at less than 5% strain with
fracture of the specimens occurring at approximately 25% strain. For the highest grayscale value
(G255), the strain at fracture is about 3% lower than the others, demonstrating a slightly reduced
ductility compared to lower light intensities. Overall, as the grayscale value increases from G51
to G153 the stiffness and strength increase dramatically before plateauing for grayscale values of
G204 and higher. This is also reflected in the measured elastic modulus values at different light
intensities shown in Figure 7-1d. The elastic modulus was determined using ASTM standard D638
for testing the tensile properties of plastics. Measured values increase almost linearly from around
10 to 200 MPa before plateauing around 350 to 400 MPa at higher light intensities. These results
demonstrate that a large range of mechanical properties can be achieved by simply adjusting the
light intensity within the projected mask image of a single layer.

 93

7.2. Inverse Problem Formulation

We aim to solve the inverse problem of obtaining light intensity maps that provide extremized
nonlinear and linearized responses in soft metamaterials when printed with grayscale DLP. To do
so, we treat this inverse problem as a PDE-constrained optimization problem of the form given in
Eq. 4.7. The design variables 𝝓 are defined at the nodes of a finite element mesh and then projected
within individual elements using a standard filtering approach from the topology optimization
literature [114]. This approach also provides some control over the sharpness of material gradients
by averaging the design variables within a radius controlled by a minimum length scale. For all
examples in this work, this length scale is set to be 2 times the average element edge length in the
mesh.

7.2.1. Light-Intensity-Aware Material Model

To model the mechanical response as a function of light intensity, we utilize a constitutive model
whose parameters vary based on the element-wise design variable value 𝜙 𝑒 . In the PDE-constrained
optimization problem Eq. 4.7, design variables are bound between values of 0 and 1. Thus, within
each element the design variable value 𝜙 𝑒 is first linearly mapped to a light intensity percentage
𝐼 (𝜙 𝑒 ) between 20 % and 100 %. Light intensity values are then smoothly mapped to Youngs
modulus values by fitting the data in Figure 7-1d with a sigmoid function. The resulting equation
is

                                                 1 − 𝑒 𝑐1 ·(𝐼 (𝜙𝑒 )−𝑐2 )
  
                                𝐸 (𝜙 𝑒 ) = 𝑐 0 ·                           + 𝑐3                    (7.1)
                                                 1 + 𝑒 𝑐1 ·(𝐼 (𝜙𝑒 )−𝑐2 )
where 𝑐 0 = 196.0684, 𝑐 1 = −0.1056, 𝑐 2 = 54.5464, and 𝑐 3 = 228.178. This Young’s modulus
value is then used to compute bulk and shear moduli for a Neo-Hookean model with the form shown
in Eq. 2.12 using a constant Poisson’s ratio 𝜈 = 0.48. It is clear from the tensile bar data in Figure 7-
1c that the materials produced by grayscale DLP are not perfectly elastic, as they show pronounced
yielding and fracture. As such, a Neo-Hookean model won’t capture essential phenomena exhibited
by these materials. Nevertheless, this model does allow us to capture the influence of local material
stiffness variations on the large deformation response of soft metamaterials produced by grayscale
DLP which – as will be demonstrated through the examples in Section 7.3 – is the primary
mechanism driving their buckling and self-contact response.

7.3. Results

Two geometries are chosen to illustrate the utility of our optimization-based design approach: (1)
the square array of circular pores from Section 3.4, and (2) the square truss lattice unit cell from
Section 3.5. Both geometries are subject to uniaxial compression using the boundary conditions
described in Sections 3.4 and 3.5. The square array of circular pores is subject to an applied
compressive displacement of 5 mm (10 % strain) over 40 equally spaced time steps. This strain is
large enough to induce a buckling-driven pattern transformation but does not induce self-contact.

 94

The square lattice unit cell is subject to an applied compressive displacement of either 2 mm (20
% strain) over 40 equally spaced time steps or 4 mm (40 % strain) over 100 equally spaced time
steps. The former induces buckling but not self-contact, while the latter induces widespread self
contact, and both will be used to highlight different behaviors. All optimization runs were carried
out using the Kelley-Sachs trust region algorithm implemented in the Rapid Optimization Library
(ROL), as described in Section 4.5. In all cases, the initial design variable vector 𝝓 contained a
uniform value of 0.5, corresponding to 60 % light intensity.

Optimized light intensity maps were translated to grayscale images and directly used to print the soft
metamaterial samples. To evaluate the compressive response of as-printed samples, compression
analysis (UTM Mark-10, Instron Instrument) was performed using a target displacement of 5 mm
and a displacement rate of 1 mm/min. Camera imaging was performed at varying strains to depict
the progression of buckling and self-contact behavior for comparison with simulated results.

7.3.1. Optimizing Nonlinear Response

We aim to control the nonlinear compressive response of soft metamaterial samples by finding light
intensity maps that extremize their crush force efficiency, as measured by Eq. 4.5. As described
in Section 4.3, crush force efficiency is a measure of how closely the response of a metamaterial
resembles an ideal energy absorber. To better understand this quantity, recall the force-displacement
response shown in Figures 3-6b and 3-7b. These exhibit three distinct phases: (1) a linear initial
response, (2) a post-buckling plateau, and (3) a rapid stiffening as the metamaterial densifies. An
ideal energy absorbing material involves a constant post-yield stress plateau maintained at a safe
threshold throughout large compressive strains [108]. As crush force efficiency measures the ratio
between the average reaction force and the peak reaction force, the maximum attainable value of
1 corresponds to a constant reaction force. Hence, maximizing this quantity will encourage a flat
post-buckling plateau that delays or eliminates densification. On the other hand, minimizing this
quantity should encourage a non-uniform post-buckling plateau and early densification.

Figure 7-2 shows the results obtained by minimizing and maximizing crush force efficiency for
both the square array of pores and the square lattice cell. The grayscale images in the left column
of Figure 7-2a and Figure 7-2c show the optimized distributions of light intensity, with lighter
regions corresponding to higher light intensity and thus higher Young’s modulus values. These
reveal the emergence of distinct regions of softer and stiffer material, often arranged in patterns
that are non-intuitive. The simulated deformed configurations are shown in the middle column of
Figure 7-2a and Figure 7-2c, and reveal that distinct buckling and self-contact responses are driven
by the different light intensity maps. Finally, the right column in Figure 7-2a and Figure 7-2c shows
snapshots of the compressive response of as-printed samples, which can be seen to mostly match
the simulated deformed configurations.

For the square array of pores, measured force-displacement responses of optimized designs are
compared to the response of a design with uniform light intensity of 60 % in Figure 7-2b. Inter-
estingly, both the uniform and maximum crush force efficiency designs display a clear softening
effect after buckling while the minimum crush force efficiency design has a stable post-buckling

 95

(a) (b)

(c) (d)

 Figure 7-2. Optimization of soft metamaterials for minimum and maximum crush force efficiency:
 (a) square array of pores with (b) corresponding measured force-displacement response; and (c)
square lattice cell with (d) corresponding measured force-displacement response. Minimum crush
 force efficiency designs are displayed above maximum crush force efficiency designs.

 96

plateau. This contrasts with the expected behavior: a stable post-buckling plateau when maxi-
mizing crush force efficiency and non-uniform post-buckling plateau when minimizing it. On the
other hand, measured force-displacement results for the optimized and uniform square lattice cell
designs match closely with the expected behavior. The maximum crush force efficiency design
has markedly reduced the post-buckling softening observed in the uniform design. The minimum
crush force efficiency design also behaves as expected: the post-buckling response is non-uniform
due to earlier self-contact. Images of the deformed configurations for these designs reveal a clear
link between the distribution of soft and stiff materials and the force-displacement response. For
instance, the minimum crush force efficiency design has a stiff region in the center with softer
regions arranged throughout the vertical and diagonal members. This causes the center region
to rigidly rotate and push diagonal members into the top and bottom of the cell, resulting in an
earlier stiffening due to self-contact. The maximum crush force efficiency design has a soft center
region and stiffer diagonal members, causing a rotation and shearing of the center region. As a
result, contact is delayed and the post-buckling response far more stable compared to the uniform
design.

 (a) (b)

Figure 7-3. Simulated force-displacement response of crush force efficiency and uniform designs:
 (a) square array of pores, (b) square lattice cell.

Figure 7-3 shows the simulated force-displacement responses of optimized and uniform designs.
Compared to the measured force-displacement responses in Figure 7-2b and Figure 7-2d, clear
discrepancies can be seen. These are attributed mostly to the lack of inelastic response in the
material model underestimating the amount of material softening that occurs as the specimens
undergo large localized strains during buckling. As such, it explains the unexpected results for
the square array of pores. What are stable post-buckling plateaus in the simulations of uniform
and maximum crush force efficiency designs exhibit marked softening when printed. Along the
same lines an almost linear simulated force-displacement response for the minimum crush force
efficiency design exhibits a clear change in stiffness after buckling when printed. The simulated
and measured force-displacement responses for the square lattice cell, on the other hand, bear much
closer qualitative resemblance, at least for the optimized designs. The earlier self-contact exhibited
by the minimum crush force efficiency design and the more stable post-buckling plateau exhibited
by the maximum crush force efficiency design are clearly observed.

 97

Despite the quantitative differences in force-displacement responses due to the lack of material
inelasticity in the model, the simulated and measured deformed configurations match extremely
closely for the most part. The one outlier is the maximum crush force efficiency design for the square
array of pores, where a different buckling polarization is observed in the simulated and measured
deformed configurations. Close observation of the as-printed design shows fracturing around the
center pores as it begins to buckle. It is possible that this fracture weakens some ligaments and
causes the design to snap into a bifurcated solution branch at lower energy. Indeed, the grayscale
image shows a distinct lack of contrast between soft and stiff regions and what differences that do
exist are difficult to discern by eye. It is likely that the optimizer took advantage of a complex
bifurcation landscape around the initial uniform design, using small perturbations in local material
properties to jump to a more optimal bifurcated solution branch. This is similar to what was
observed with this square array of pores when carrying out shape optimization, see Figure 5-13.
Such minor perturbations in light intensity are susceptible to manufacturing imperfections. By
contrast, the other designs show distinct regions of high and low light intensity which are more
tolerant to manufacturing imperfections. As a result, the as-printed designs deform in a remarkably
similar way to the simulated designs.

7.3.2. Optimizing Linearized Response

Next, we aim to control the linearized vibration response in soft metamaterial samples by ex-
tremizing the difference between the lowest natural frequency in the deformed and undeformed
configurations. To do so, we use the scalar quantity of interest in Eq. 4.6 as our objective for
optimization-based design. For both the square array of pores and the square lattice cell, the
applied displacement is set to a value where self-contact should be minimal. Additionally, it should
be noted that both geometries contain only simple eigenvalues so the sensitivity approach discussed
in Section 4.4.1 is appropriate. By minimizing the difference in eigenvalues, the lowest natural
frequency in the deformed configuration should be close to the lowest natural frequency in the
undeformed configuration. On the other hand, maximizing it will cause the lowest natural fre-
quency in the deformed configuration to be far from the lowest natural frequency in the undeformed
configuration.

Figure 7-4 shows the results obtained by minimizing and maximizing the eigenvalue difference
for both the square array of pores and the square lattice cell. The first thing that stands out is the
remarkable agreement between the simulated and measured deformed configurations. Grayscale
images show that distinct regions of high and low light intensity exist in all designs, making them
more imperfection tolerant and likely attributing to the close agreement in deformed configurations.
For this objective, it is more difficult to intuit why certain distributions of stiff and soft material
emerge, as the link between buckling patterns and linearized vibration response is not as clear as
it is for crush force efficiency. However, the simulated. evolution of the first natural frequency
over the applied displacement (preload) is plotted in Figure 7-5 for the optimized and uniform
designs. Here, it can clearly be seen that maximizing or minimizing the difference in natural
frequency between deformed and undeformed configurations causes a clear change in response.
Recall from the discussion in Section 3.6 that the frequency drops near the buckling critical point.
By minimizing the eigenvalue difference, this drop in frequency is reduced for both the square array

 98

(a) (b)

(c) (d)

 Figure 7-4. Optimization of soft metamaterials for minimum and maximum difference between the
lowest natural frequency in the deformed and undeformed configurations (a) square array of pores
 with (b) corresponding measured force-displacement response; and (c) square lattice cell with (d)
 corresponding measured force-displacement response. Maximum eigenvalue difference designs
 are displayed above minimum eigenvalue difference designs.

 99

of pores and the square lattice cell designs. Maximizing the eigenvalue difference for the square
array of pores causes this drop in frequency to continue after the critical point is reached. In contrast,
maximizing the eigenvalue difference for the square lattice cell causes the drop in frequency to be
minor and the subsequent increase in frequency to be significant. As a result, the natural frequency
in the deformed configuration is much higher than it is in the undeformed configuration for the
square lattice cell and vice versa for the square array of pores. Overall, these results show that a
wide range of linearized vibration responses can be obtained in soft metamaterials by controlling
the layout of soft and stiff regions.

  Figure 7-5. Simulated evolution of the first natural frequency during preload for optimized and
 uniform designs: (a) square array of pores, (b) square lattice cell.

7.4. Conclusions

In this chapter, an optimization-based design approach was presented to exploit the large design
space afforded by grayscale DLP for tailoring the nonlinear and linearized response of soft meta-
materials. Overall, it was shown that controlling the distribution of soft and stiff phases throughout
a metamaterial structure results in markedly different buckling and self-contact responses. By
optimizing these distributions, we were able to print metamaterial samples with extremized non-
linear and linearized properties. Quantitative discrepancies in the simulated and measured force-
displacement response were observed due to the lack of material inelasticity in the simulations.
This manifested as a markedly softer post-buckling response in the as-printed samples. Despite
this, remarkable agreement was shown between the buckling and self-contact response in simulated
and measured deformed configurations. This work focused on using optimization-based design to
exploit varying material stiffness, but the approach can be extended to include inelastic effects such
as shape-memory properties and mechanical hysteresis. Such effects could be useful for designing
deployable metamaterials or soft actuators with functionally optimized material distributions.

 100

8. INSTABILITIES AND PHASE TRANSFORMATIONS IN
 METAMATERIALS AND ARCHITECTED MATERIALS: A
 GRADIENT-ENHANCED CONTINUUM APPROACH

In the preceding chapters we demonstrated the use of optimization-based design techniques to
tailor the nonlinear and linearized response of soft metamaterials undergoing instabilities and
self-contact that result in large configurational changes. These chapters demonstrated how such an
approach can be used to tailor fine-scale geometric features to induce the desired nonlinear responses
and configurational changes, but are limited to relatively small material samples rather than full
component-scale geometries. The computational cost of considering multi-scale nonlinear solid
mechanics phenomena in components synthesized from architected and porous materials becomes
prohibitively expensive, especially if it is to be included in an iterative optimiation loop. Instead,
continuum models are often employed at the component scale by assuming that the scale separation
between geometric features in the material architecture and dimensions in the component is large
enough to treat the material as a continuum.
The unconventional responses displayed by soft metamaterials are often rooted in microstructural
instabilities, and non-affine micro-deformation mechanisms that govern the redistribution of strain
and stress across the structure [9, 84], through the emergence of apparent phase transformations
(e.g. a transition between a rare and dense phase, or one where the microstructure has undergone
buckling instabilities). However, local continuum formulations are fundamentally limited in their
ability to capture such phase transitions [247], which are crucial for the development of continuum
theories that could be predictive for macroscopic simulations without having to resort to resolving
the complete microstructural details [190, 53, 22, 29, 25, 103, 39]. Additionally, they fail to
capture snap-through instabilities, softening transitions, and hysteretic behavior, and often produce
mesh-dependent results when localization is observed. Although computational homogenization,
where a representative volume element (RVE) is utilized to obtain a local model, is often used for
problems in architected materials and metamaterials [105], the resulting models eventually suffer
from the same deficiencies. Additionally, if the RVE localizes it alludes to multiple localizations
in the microscale due to periodicity assumptions, and as such, the RVE that has to be considered
is the infinite RVE to capture the energy landscape for the phase transition. There have been some
successful models that capture the deformation mechanism at the RVE level [151, 152] but these
do not aim to capture sharp transitions, nor can they generalize to other types of materials.
Early attention on modeling of metallic foams focused on the effects of yielding and hardening
developing local constitutive models [80, 79, 81], but the importance of accounting for material
length scales in this type of continuum models was also identified early [52], inspired from previ-
ously introduced strain gradient plasticity models [98]. Micromorphic and generalized continuum
models introduce characteristic length scales to regularize localization [99, 63], and while their
complexity often is limiting for the development of interpretable models, there have been recent

 101

efforts in this direction related to metamaterials [238, 115, 181, 251]. Even though there have been
significant developments, a general formulation that can enable capturing the macroscopic response
but also the emerging phase transitions that occur in architected materials and metamaterials is still
lacking.
Historically, constitutive modeling of architected materials exhibiting instabilities has largely been
carried out in the context of plasticity, where yield surfaces and hardening rules have been used
to approximate irreversible deformation and phase transitions. However, these plasticity-based
approaches typically lack thermodynamic consistency and fail to resolve sharp transitions or elas-
tic recoverability. Despite their limitation, local elasticity models have also been used to model
instabilities and phase transformations. To do so, they often incorporate non-(poly)convex free
energy, permitting multiple local minima in the energy landscape [104]. Building on Ericksen’s
foundational work on non-monotonic stress–strain relations [89], various phase transition models
have been proposed [1]. Further, loss of ellipticity [154], is a common indicator for instabilities,
that can be triggered by various deformation mechanisms, including purely elastic deformations
in composites [177], but also plasticity [3]. These instabilities can often also lead to the emer-
gence of phase transformations [102, 132], triggered from various mechanisms, including shear
deformation.
Yet, in the absence of regularization, local non-(poly)convex models often suffer from mesh
sensitivity [189, 17]. To overcome these limitations, gradient-enhanced extensions have been
developed, wherein the non-(poly)convex free energy is augmented by gradient penalties acting
on internal variables—such as averaged invariants or non-local volume ratios [224, 21, 67, 131,
194]. These terms suppress spurious localization, introduce a material length scale, and allow for
physically meaningful solutions. Phase field models can also be viewed as a sub-class of these
methods, and are often used to model phase transformations, introducing an order parameter and
utilizing free energy formulations that allow for multiple local minima [56, 38, 165].
On the design front, advances in homogenization [44, 85], concurrent multiscale modeling [273,
253, 14], and topology optimization [113, 274, 20, 107, 206, 50] have opened pathways for
embedding instabilities into functional materials. Data-driven and neural-network-based methods
offer accelerated design tools [160, 288, 245, 147, 129], and learning approaches [145, 101], though
challenges remain in generalizing these approaches across diverse loading conditions, geometries,
and ensuring thermodynamic admissibility.
In this work, we develop a thermodynamically consistent, gradient-enhanced continuum finite
element framework for modeling instabilities, phase transitions, and auxetic responses in architected
materials and metamaterials. To construct the non-local model, we enhance local polyconvex
models with two families of non-(poly)convex energies which additionally incorporate length
scales relevant to the microstructure. This allows the model to exhibit metastable and bistable
responses in constrained compression experiments. Spatial regularization is achieved through
gradient penalties, and artificial viscosity is introduced. The framework is implemented via finite
elements and a hybrid solution strategy is implemented utilizing both monolithic and staggered
approaches. A model is specialized for phase transitions between a dense and a rare phase,
and numerical simulations demonstrate the emergence of densification fronts, forward and reverse
transformations, hysteresis loops, tunable imperfection sensitivity, and globally coordinated auxetic
modes.

 102

The remainder of the chapter is organized as follows. In Section 8.1, we introduce a framework
for anisotropic hyperelasticity, further non-(poly)convex free energy formulations are introduced
to connect to phase transformations, and finally, these models are specialized to deliver auxetic
response. Section 8.2 develops the non-local extension using a gradient-enhanced model with
internal variables related to mixed invariants introduced for anisotropic hyperelasticity. Further,
the model is specialized for an isotropic case to account for instabilities that are governed by the
volume ratio. Section 8.3 outlines the finite element implementation, including discretization,
nonlinear solution strategies, and mixed space construction. Section 8.4 presents simulations to
examine densification fronts, hysteresis, graded imperfection sensitivity, and emergent auxeticity.
Conclusions and future directions are provided in Section 8.5.

8.1. A Local Description of Instabilities and Phase Transitions

8.1.1. A framework for anisotropic hyperelasticity

Consider an elastic body occupying the reference configuration Ω0 ⊂ R3 . Let the boundary of the
body 𝜕Ω0 be composed of two parts 𝜕Ω𝑡0 and 𝜕Ω𝑢0 such that 𝜕Ω0 = 𝜕Ω𝑡0 ∪ 𝜕Ω𝑢0 . Here, 𝜕Ω𝑢0 and 𝜕Ω𝑡0
describe the boundary sections that displacement and traction boundary conditions are prescribed.
The motion between the referential position X and the current position x can be defined by

                                          x = 𝜑(X, 𝑡) = X + u(X, 𝑡)                                           (8.1)

where u describes the time-dependent displacement field and 𝜑(X, 𝑡) denotes the motion of the
body. This allows to define the deformation gradient

                                                   F = ∇𝜑(X)                                                  (8.2)

In the hyperelastic framework, existence of the free energy density function Ψ is postulated, which
is assumed to be defined per unit reference volume [127]. The formulation of a free energy density
function is dependent on the symmetry group that complies with the symmetries that correspond
to a specific material. A symmetry group of a material is a set of transformations that allow
for material symmetry to be preserved. If a scalar-valued tensor function, such as the strain
energy density function, is invariant under a rotation it may be written in terms of the invariants
(𝐼1 , . . . , 𝐼n ) of its arguments as Ψ(𝐼1 , . . . , 𝐼n ) [127]. For purely mechanical processes of perfectly
elastic materials, the second law of thermodynamics requires that the Clausius-Planck inequality
turns into an equality, leading to
 n
                                      𝜕Ψ(𝐼1 , . . . , 𝐼n )    ∑︁  𝜕Ψ 𝜕𝐼𝑖
                                  S=2                      =2            .                                    (8.3)
 𝜕C 𝑖=1
 𝜕𝐼𝑖 𝜕C

where S, the second Piola-Kirchhoff stress tensor, C = F𝑇 F is the right Cauchy-Green deformation
tensor, (𝐼1 , . . . , 𝐼3 ) are its principal invariants, and (𝐼4 , . . . , 𝐼n ) are the so-called pseudo-invariants.

 103

Commonly, hyperelastic constitutive laws are constructed so that the reference configuration cor-
responds to a stress-free state with zero energy. This can be achieved by requiring that

                                             Ψ(I, •) = 0                                         (8.4)

in the reference configuration where F = C = I. This condition and the physically sound assumption
that the strain energy function increases under deformation, i.e. Ψ(C, •) ≥ 0, ensures that the stress
in the reference configuration is zero, i.e.

                                             S(I, •) = 0                                         (8.5)

Additionally, the growth condition ensures coercivity of the strain energy density and is typically
formulated by requiring that

                        Ψ(C, •) → ∞ as        det(C) → 0 or       ∥C∥ → ∞,                       (8.6)

which guarantees that the material response remains physically meaningful under extreme volu-
metric deformations. Finally, Ball [23] introduced the concept of polyconvexity as a sufficient
condition for ensuring the existence of minimizers in nonlinear elasticity. A function Ψ(F) is said
to be polyconvex if it can be expressed as a function ℎ, that is individually convex with respect to
its arguments F, det F, and cof F. That is,

                                     Ψ(F) = ℎ(F, cof F, det F).                                  (8.7)

Polyconvexity also implies rank-one convexity which in turn ensures that ellipticity is preserved,
preventing short-wavelength instabilities.

8.1.2. Non-(poly)convex models

Extensive theoretical investigations have been dedicated to understanding phase transitions and
twinning phenomena in solid materials [24]. Central to this effort is the formulation of energy
functionals incorporating non-(poly)convex free energy densities, which serve as prototypical mod-
els for capturing the emergence of multiple stable or metastable states within the material [104].
Pioneering work by Ericksen [89] elucidated the implications of non-(poly)convex energy land-
scapes by analyzing an elastic bar governed by a non-monotonic stress-strain relation. His results
revealed the existence of multiple non-unique minimizers, corresponding to distinct equilibrium
configurations separated by sharp discontinuities. These discontinuities are interpreted as phase
interfaces—transition zones across which the material switches between coexisting phases. The
presence of such interfaces is crucial for interpreting experimental manifestations of hysteresis, as
material points become arrested in metastable states during loading-unloading cycles.
Building upon the foundational framework developed by Abeyaratne and Knowles [1] in the context
of hyperelasticity, a nonlinearly elastic material can be characterized through the Helmholtz free
energy density function Ψ(𝛾), representing the strain energy per unit reference volume as a function
of the strain measure 𝛾. The associated stress response is given by:
 𝑑Ψ
 𝜎 (𝛾) =
 𝜎=b . (8.8)
 𝑑𝛾

 104

On the other hand, the Gibbs free energy (referred to as potential energy in [1]) per unit reference
volume, incorporating the work of external stress, is expressed as:
                                        b 𝜎) = Ψ(𝛾) − 𝜎𝛾.
 Ψ(𝛾, (8.9)

and is simply a Legendre transform of the Helmholtz free energy, moving from a displacement-
control to a force-control setting. The identification of extrema in Ψ(·,
 b 𝜎) is therefore central
to predicting phase transitions, as each local minimum represents a metastable or stable phase
configuration. When multiple such minima exist, with distinct energy barriers, the system can
exhibit spatial phase segregation, which are hallmarks of materials with rich internal energy
landscapes.
Such a scenario necessitates a non-(poly)convex form of Ψ(𝛾), resulting in a stress-strain rela-
tionship that is inherently non-monotonic. This behavior underlies the phase coexistence and
transition mechanisms observed in numerous soft and crystalline materials. In the broader con-
text of architected and biological materials, these insights form the theoretical foundation for
modeling metastability, structural reorganization, and hysteresis as emergent phenomena driven
by energy minimization in non-(poly)convex energy landscapes. This framework not only ex-
plains mechanical instabilities but also provides a rigorous basis for tracking the evolution of
interfaces between competing phases through variational methods. Importantly, the presence of
a non-monotonic stress-strain relation necessitates a generalized treatment of equilibrium, where
conventional uniqueness and convexity assumptions break down.
By formalizing the mechanical response of materials through non-convex potentials and their cor-
responding minimization principles, we gain a powerful lens to interpret localized instabilities and
phase propagation phenomena. This theoretical scaffolding sets the stage for advanced constitutive
modeling.

8.1.3. Instabilities inducing auxetic response

In metamaterials, and architected materials more generally, auxetic response is often encountered
and is typically the result of microstructural deformation mechanisms that trigger a cascade that
spans microscopic and macroscopic length scales [54]. In such auxetic responses, significant
densification is observed at the microstructure level (the spatial density of the material is increasing).
In an idealized case in isotropic hyperelasticity we can consider that the transition from a rare to a
dense phase and vice versa, is controlled just by the dependence of the free energy on the volume
ratio 𝐽 = 𝑑𝑒𝑡 (F) (as it relates to the third principal invariant of the right Cauchy-Green deformation
tensor C). We aim to distinguish between volumetric transformations associated with material
densification and rarefaction. Specifically, the transition from a reference configuration (𝐽 = 1)
to a densified state (𝐽 < 1) through a phase transition naturally invokes a non-convex formulation
of the free energy, mimicking the behavioral pathway observed in compression-induced structural
transformations.
The base constitutive model adopted in this work is the compressible Neo-Hookean formulation.
This simple model captures nonlinearities associated with soft solids and enables the representation
of finite compressibility effects. To account for unstable auxetic behavior, we build upon the

 105

formulation proposed by Gao and Ogden [104] (with a change of variable from 𝛾 to 𝐽) and also
the double-well formulation often used in phase-field theory. The corresponding local form of the
proposed free energy density is defined via an additive decomposition:

                                          Ψ = ΨC + ΨNC                                      (8.10)

where the first term ΨC is the compressible Neo-Hookean contribution, and the second term
ΨNC represents a non-(poly)convex component, chosen either as the Gao–Ogden model ΨGO or a
double-well form ΨDW . These are given by:

Here, 𝜇 and 𝜅 denote the shear and bulk moduli, 𝛼 and 𝛽 are tuning parameters controlling non-

model.
To examine the energy landscape defined by Eq. 8.10 and Eq. 8.12a for the Gao-Ogden model,
we consider a hydrostatic deformation with principal stretch 𝜆, so that 𝐽 = 𝜆3 and 𝐼1 = 3𝜆2 .

the undeformed state, to a landscape with two local minima (one at the undeformed configuration
𝐽 = 1, and one at high compression 𝐽 ≈ 0.35).

While the local formulation captures the key characteristics of phase transitions and densification
under hydrostatic compression, it overlooks spatial interactions and the role of microstructural
gradients. However, in many physical systems the mechanical response is governed by inherently
non-local phenomena. To account for these effects, we extend the framework to a non-local
formulation in a gradient-enhanced setting. This regularization not only enables the resolution of
diffuse phase boundaries but also mitigates unphysical localization, yielding a more stable, accurate,
and physically meaningful representation of material behavior.

8.2. A Non-Local Description for Architected Materials

Even though our construction for a non-(poly)convex free energy density in Section 8.1.3 focused
on the volumetric response, here a more general framework will be presented that could allow
extensions to anisotropic architected materials. This aims to allow for phase transformations and
multistability in the context of anisotropic hyperelasticity. Additionally, to introduce a physically
meaningful connection to the underlying microstructural features, we will extend to a non-local
formulation. This non-locality allows the model to capture long-range spatial interactions that are
essential for resolving the emerging phase-boundary topology at a macroscopic level. In particular,
instabilities arising due to the non-(poly)convex local formulation presented in Section 8.1.3 could
be recast in terms of a non-local counterpart of the Jacobian determinant, denoted 𝐽.  ˜ Equivalently,
the non-local counterparts for the mixed invariants could also be considered as ( 𝐼˜1 , . . . , 𝐼˜n ).

8.2.1. Thermodynamic considerations

We begin by identifying the relevant state variables governing the system. The primary kinematic
variable is the displacement field u, which defines the deformation of the material. To capture non-
local effects and regularize the phase transitions, we augment the state space with a non-local mixed
invariants ( 𝐼˜1 , . . . , 𝐼˜n ). Together, u and ( 𝐼˜1 , . . . , 𝐼˜n ) provide a comprehensive set of state variables
capable of capturing both the macroscopic deformation and the microstructural evolution associated
with phase transformation in anisotropic architected materials. One can physically interpret these
non-local quantities as corresponding mixed invariants averaged through a representative volume
relevant to the micro-architecture of the material. With these state variables defined, we employ
the principle of virtual power to establish the governing equations.

 108

The internal mechanical power, 𝑃int , in the reference configuration Ω0 , is expressed as

8.2.4. Strong and weak Forms

Similar to the phase field model for fracture [189, 17], in this work we aim to capture unstable
transitions of a materials system. Anticipating the potential convergence issues of the numerical
solution, we incorporate an artificial viscosity term that introduces a controlled dissipation mech-
anism by penalizing the rapid temporal evolution of non-local variables (in this case the non-local
volume ratio), thereby enhancing the stability and robustness of the numerical solution. This
damping mechanism is artificial, but it is often taken to be representative with dissipation related
to the rate of the micro-mechanism in consideration. Below, we revert to the general notation for
our formulation.

Strong Form. The governing equations, modified to include viscous damping, are:

 • Field Equations:

                                                          ∇·P=0          in Ω0 ,                (8.30a)

 • Boundary Conditions:

                                                  u = ǔ on 𝜕Ω𝑢0 ,                              (8.31a)
                                              P · N = T on 𝜕Ω𝑡0 ,                               (8.31b)
                                                  𝐼˜𝑖 = 𝐼ˇ𝑖 on 𝜕Ω0𝐼𝑖 ,                          (8.31c)
 𝜉
                                            𝝃 𝐼˜𝑖 · N = 𝜄ˇ𝑖    on 𝜕Ω0𝑖 .                        (8.31d)

Here, 𝜂𝑖 > 0 is the artificial viscosity coefficient associated with the 𝑖-th non-local mixed invariant,
and 𝐼¤̃𝑖 denotes its rate of change over time. The introduction of artificial viscosity serves a dual
purpose: it improves numerical stability in the presence of steep gradients in the internal field,
𝐼˜𝑖 and provides a consistent means of regulating the incremental evolution of non-local mixed
invariants through a dissipative-like mechanism.

8.3. Finite Element Implementation

The proposed computational framework is implemented using the open-source platform FEniCS
[176, 15], which facilitates the concise definition and efficient evaluation of variational forms.
Automated differentiation via the Unified Form Language (UFL) [16], coupled with PETSc-based
solver backends, enables consistent linearization and robust handling of nonlinear systems. All
simulation scripts are made available in a public GitHub repository.1

The governing equations are posed over the reference domain Ω0 ⊂ R𝑑 and involve two primary
fields: the displacement u : Ω0 → R𝑑 , and non-local volume ratio 𝐽˜ : Ω0 → R, which captures
volumetric softening and gradient-regularized phase transitions. The total potential is defined via a
non-(poly)convex Helmholtz free energy functional incorporating both classical strain energy and
gradient-penalized microforces.

8.3.1. Discretization and function Spaces

A mixed finite element strategy is employed to resolve the coupled field problem. We choose a
Taylor-Hood space, where the displacement u is discretized using quadratic Lagrange elements,
while 𝐽˜ is approximated with linear elements.
 1 GitHub link to be provided upon publication.

 113

8.3.2. Solution strategy

The discrete system is solved via a Newton–Raphson procedure applied to the coupled residual.
Artificial viscosity is introduced to regularize the evolution of 𝐽˜ in pseudo-time. This regularization
is discretized using a backward Euler scheme, an implicit first-order time integration method. At
each pseudo-time step, the evolution of 𝐽˜ is computed using its current value and its value from
the previous step, treating the update as fully implicit. The backward Euler method is particularly
well-suited for these problems, as it suppresses high-frequency numerical oscillations and enables
stable convergence across a wide range of parameter regimes.

Nonlinear systems are solved using PETSc’s SNES interface with line search globalization and
MUMPS direct solver backends, augmented by Hypre algebraic multigrid (AMG) preconditioners.
Convergence is monitored via relative and absolute residual norms, with tolerances set to 10−9 , and
a maximum of 50 iterations per step. These criteria enable accurate resolution of sharp transitions
while maintaining computational efficiency.

Further, a hybrid scheme is proposed. When convergence is not attained in the coupled system,
the algorithm reverts to a staggered scheme 2 . In this alternate strategy, the displacement u and
non-local volume ratio 𝐽˜ are solved sequentially: first, u is updated for fixed 𝐽,
 ˜ and subsequently
𝐽˜ is evolved using the current configuration of u. This alternating minimization proceeds until the
update norm satisfies

                                         ∥ 𝐽˜(𝑘+1) − 𝐽˜(𝑘) ∥ 𝐿 2 (Ω0 ) < 𝜖stag ,

with 𝜖stag = 10−3 . The staggered approach significantly improves robustness in non-(poly)convex
regimes, allowing the simulation to proceed through regions of severe instability while maintaining
fidelity to the governing equations.

 2 It is noted that this is opposite from the strategy often followed in phase field fracture problems, where first a

 staggered scheme is followed and the solution scheme reverts to monolithic upon satisfactory reduction of the
 residual.

 114

 1: Generate mesh on Ω0 ; define function spaces U, J, and mixed space V = U × J
 2: Initialize u0 , 𝐽˜0
 3: for each load step 𝑖 = 1, . . . , 𝑁 do
 (𝑖)
 4: Apply increment 𝑢 inc
 5: Solve R (u, 𝐽)˜ = 0 via monolithic Newton method (SNES)
 6: if converged then
 7: Record solution; compute derived quantities (stress, 𝐽, Ψ, etc.)
 8: else
 9: Initialize staggered fields: u (0) ← u𝑖−1 , 𝐽˜(0) ← 𝐽˜𝑖−1
  10: repeat
  11: Solve for u (𝑘+1) with fixed 𝐽˜(𝑘)
  12: Solve for 𝐽˜(𝑘+1) with fixed u (𝑘+1)
  13:        until ∥ 𝐽˜(𝑘+1) − 𝐽˜(𝑘) ∥ 𝐿 2 (Ω0 ) < 𝜖stag
