Newton solver with a staggered approach.

8.4. Results and Discussion

This section presents a detailed assessment of the proposed non-local finite element framework
through a series of numerical simulations designed to evaluate its physical fidelity and compu-
tational robustness. The simulations investigate the mechanical response of architected solids
under displacement-controlled monotonic and cyclic loading, with a focus on capturing phase
transformations and instabilities.
Fig. 8-4 outlines the simulation setup. Fig. 8-4(a) corresponds to a unit square domain that
undergoes confined compression in plane strain; this setup is utilized for the majority of the results.
A flat, frictionless indenter imposes vertical displacement on the top surface, while roller boundary
conditions along the lateral and bottom edges are applied. In cyclic simulations, the indenter is
not imposed through a prescribed displacement but through a contact constraint, this is crucial
for identifying potential equilibrium states during the unloading. Fig. 8-4(b) illustrates the spatial
variation of the bulk modulus 𝜅(X) that is utilized for some simulation. It is implemented as a
linear gradient along the domain height 𝑦 (𝑦 = 0 at the bottom face) with a prescribed percentage
deviation around a maximum value (e.g., 5% about 𝜅 = 2.0).
Two representative material models are considered to construct a metastable and a bistable case. In
both cases (all quantities are dimensionless), the shear modulus 𝜇 and the maximum bulk modulus 𝜅
are set to 2.0. The metastable case uses 𝛼 = 300.0, 𝛽 = 0.5, producing a shallow single-well energy
landscape that allows for reversible phase transformations. The bistable case adopts 𝛼 = 1000.0,
𝛽 = 0.35, yielding a double-well profile with a secondary energy minimum that supports permanent
deformation post-unloading. The reaction force 𝐹¯ is normalized by the shear modulus 𝜇, and the

 115

  Figure 8-4. Simulation setup and imposed inhomogeneity. (a) Loading configuration for both
 monotonic and cyclic cases. (b) Spatial variation of the bulk modulus 𝜅(𝑦), shown for a 5% linear
 grading.

vertical displacement 𝛿 by the domain height. The non-local length scale that we select, normalized
by the height of the domain is 𝑙/𝐻 = 0.017, and is considered to be relevant to the representative
volume element (RVE) or statistical volume element (SVE) size.
The subsections that follow investigate key modeling features in depth: solver verification and
effects of artificial viscosity, imperfection sensitivity under stiffness grading, spatial evolution of
local and non-local volumetric fields, cyclic response with reversibility and retention, artificial
viscosity-driven softening behavior, and the emergence of auxetic patterns under strong non-local
coupling.

8.4.1. Performance of the hybrid solution scheme

We begin by examining the influence of the artificial viscosity parameter 𝜂 using a fixed structured
mesh. Fig. 8-5 presents the force–displacement response for both selected cases for the metastable
and bistable models under varying values of 𝜂, with a fixed vertical bulk modulus variation of 5.0%.
Overall the response is separated in three stages i) elastic loading, ii) plateau, and iii) densification.
For artificial viscosity 𝜂 < 1 the hybrid solver resorts to the staggered scheme to reach convergence,
whereas for 𝜂 ≥ 1 convergence is attained through a monolithic scheme. Higher values of artificial
viscosity lead to significantly faster convergence. During elastic loading a limit load is reached,
followed by a plateau with prominent oscillations indicative of instabilities, and eventually a stable
densification path. This response is indicative of the elastic response of foams and low relative
density materials. For low artificial viscosity, the solutions exhibits a sharp drop after the limit
load, which is smoothened for higher values. To maintain the important features of the solution
profile and take advantage of the acceleration provided when adopting higher values of artificial
viscosity, we utilize an intermediate value of 𝜂 = 5.0 was found to provide an effective balance
between physical fidelity and acceleration. It is important to note that artificial viscosity is often

 116

taken to mimic micro-level dissipation associated with rate effects for the evolution of the non-local
variables.

Figure 8-5. Effect of artificial viscosity 𝜂 on the force-displacement response for (a) metastable and
 (b) bistable energy landscapes. Simulations correspond to a bulk modulus variation of 𝜅 by 5.0%
 along the height of the domain.

Further, we investigate the sensitivity of the solution to mesh topology, comparing structured and
unstructured triangular meshes for a fixed viscosity 𝜂 = 5.0 and a vertical bulk modulus variation
of 1.0%.

 Figure 8-6. Comparison of force-displacement responses obtained using structured and
 unstructured meshes. Results correspond to a bulk modulus variation of 𝜅 by 1.0% along the
 domain height and viscosity 𝜂 = 5.0.

Fig. 8-6 shows that both mesh types successfully capture key mechanical features, including
stiffness softening and onset of internal transitions. Minor variations in the unstable plateau stage
are attributed to mesh-induced heterogeneity of the solution. One can note that the unstructured
mesh leads to a smoother plateau compared to the structured case. The increased heterogeneity
and approximation error expected in the unstructured case lead to local response-type (instead of
limit load-type) behavior during the plateau. In reality, imperfection sensitivity in these materials
is critical (as will be further discussed), and in homogeneous numerical cases, these imperfections
lie in the spatial fluctuations due to numerical approximation error.

 117

These findings collectively underscore the effectiveness of the proposed formulation in delivering
stable and physically consistent results across a range of discretization schemes and regularization
parameters. Building on this, we now investigate how spatial heterogeneity of material properties
–introduced through bulk modulus grading– can further influence the onset and progression of
localized phase transitions.

8.4.2. Imperfection sensitivity and graded structural properties

We introduce a controlled vertical gradient in the bulk modulus 𝜅(X) across the height of the
domain. This gradient is designed such that 𝜅(X) increases from the top to the bottom of the
specimen, thereby having lower compressibility at the top as indicated by the bulk to shear ratio
𝜅/𝜇 corresponding to the Neo-Hookean model; we note that these are not the true linearized bulk
modulus of the proposed material models, but the comparison to the base Neo-Hookean model is
useful for the stable elastic loading segment of the response. Additionally, in this study, we have
not perturbed any of the parameters of the non-(poly)convex energy contribution ΨNC . This spatial
variation serves as an intentional imperfection that biases the mechanical response and encourages
preferential zones for the initiation of densification.
Fig. 8-7 illustrates the force–displacement behavior alongside snapshots of the Jacobian field 𝐽 at
five distinct loading stages, for four different levels of vertical grading: 0.0%, 1.0%, 5.0%, and
10.0%. We focus on 5 snapshots of the loading up to the end of plateau. When the material
properties are homogeneous throughout the domain (0.0% variation), the system exhibits homo-
geneous deformation during elastic loading with spontaneous densification in two distinct regions,
which later merge, and ultimately the entirety of the domain is densified. The location of the initial
densified regions is purely due to imperfection sensitivity to the noise due to the approximation
error. The introduction of a modest gradient (e.g., 1.0%) causes the densification front to nucleate
earlier and more distinctly near the top region, even though there is still a secondary densification
region in the lower part of the domain. In this case, two fronts propagate and ultimately merge at
the center of the domain. As the magnitude of the gradient increases, this behavior becomes more
pronounced and localized. Higher variation in 𝜅 (e.g., 5.0% and 10.0%) results in sharper front
formation, earlier observation of densification. In all cases, the emergence of densification matched
the drop in the force-displacement plot transitioning from the elastic loading stage to the plateau.
In the plateau stage, there is coexistence of a highly densified phase (𝐽 ≈ 0.3) and a rare phase
(𝐽 ≈ 0.9), indicating 70% and 10% volumetric compression accordingly. During this apparent
phase transformation the force required is stable and only starts increasing during the densification
stage.

8.4.3. Local and non-local volume ratio evolution

To investigate the evolution of local and non-local volume ratio during loading, we track the local
volumetric deformation 𝐽 and its non-local counterpart 𝐽˜ at three vertical probe points positioned
at increasing heights along the specimen’s centerline, as shown in Fig. 8-8(a). These probes are
chosen to monitor the densification through the domain under increasing compressive loading.

 118

Figure 8-7. Effect of vertical grading in bulk modulus 𝜅 on the force-displacement response and the
evolution of the Jacobian field 𝐽 for a metastable material. The graded profiles correspond to 0.0%,
 1.0%, 5.0%, and 10.0% variation in 𝜅 over the domain height. Snapshots at five loading stages
 illustrate the impact of heterogeneity of material properties on densification.

 119

 Figure 8-8. Evolution of local volume ratio 𝐽 and non-local volume ratio 𝐽˜ at three vertical probe
points for a metastable material with artificial viscosity 𝜂 = 20.0. Spatial probe locations are shown
in (a), with corresponding variable trajectories shown in (b). A slight rise in response is observed
  prior to the collapse, indicating relaxation of the underlying material as the densification front
 passes.

The plot in Fig. 8-8(b) shows the evolution of 𝐽 and 𝐽˜ as functions of the applied displacement 𝛿.
Initially, all probes exhibit a uniform, nearly linear reduction in volume due to elastic compression.
As loading continues, a sudden drop in 𝐽 is observed at each probe, indicating the onset of local
collapse as the densification front reaches that region. Just before this drop, however, a slight
increase in both 𝐽 and 𝐽˜ is noticeable. This jump reflects the relaxation of the material below
the probe as the region above densifies, causing local unloading that momentarily counteracts the
ongoing compression.
The non-local variable 𝐽˜ evolves more smoothly than its local counterpart due to spatial averaging
and possibly the influence of artificial viscosity. This smoothing effect becomes particularly evident
near the instability threshold, where 𝐽 undergoes sharp transitions, while 𝐽˜ changes more gradually.
The temporal ordering of these transitions across the probes also reflects the downward propagation
of the densification front.

8.4.4. Cyclic loading response

To examine whether our proposed models can capture the path-dependent behavior of architected
solids under repeated loading, we perform cyclic compression tests on both metastable and bistable
material systems using a flat-top indenter. Specifically, for the both the metastable and the bistable
case we have in mind the corresponding responses discussed in Chapter 5 for the snap through cell
and chain of cells and we aim to qualitatively reproduce them while also pushing the response into
the densification stage.
The indenter imposes displacement-controlled loading from the top surface, with unilateral contact
constraints governing its interaction with the deformable body. This contact-based approach is
utilized such that the indenter can detach from the body in case a secondary stable equilibrium
state is attained. This setup facilitates realistic simulation of compression and partial recovery,
capturing how deformation fronts form, propagate, and evolve under cyclic loading conditions,
with responses shaped by the underlying energy landscape and material history.

 120

Figure 8-9. Cyclic response for a metastable material system with 5.0% vertical grading in bulk
 modulus 𝜅 and artificial viscosity 𝜂 = 5.0. Displacement-controlled loading and unloading are
 applied via a flat-top indenter in contact with the top surface. The top plot shows the
force–displacement trajectory over one full cycle; the numbered snapshots correspond to key
 loading stages and show the evolution of the Jacobian field 𝐽.

 121

Fig. 8-9 illustrates the cyclic response for the metastable case. The force–displacement curve
exhibits a nearly reversible trajectory with mild hysteresis. Snapshots at 12 key points highlight the
evolution of the local volume ratio 𝐽 during loading (points 1–6) and unloading (points 7–12). As
the indenter compresses the body, a densified front emerges near the top and progresses downward
in response to increasing deformation. Upon unloading, partial recovery occurs as the front recedes
until a fully recovery to the initial state is achieved. This reversible behavior is characteristic of
metastable systems with moderate energy barriers.

In contrast, the bistable case shown in Fig. 8-10 displays a markedly different cyclic response. The
force–displacement curve reveals strong hysteresis and significant irreversibility, indicating that the
material undergoes a phase transition into a deep energy well from which it cannot fully recover
upon unloading. As the flat indenter compresses the structure (points 1–9), multiple densified
zones nucleate and ultimately merge to a single densified region. This indicates that the induced
5% grading was not strong enough to bias the densification strictly to the top region. In architected
materials this is usually a sign of heterogeneity/polydispersity. During unloading (points 10–12),
the reverse transformation does not initiate and the material reaches a secondary stable equilibrium
state, indicative of the bistable response.

Overall, these simulations demonstrate that the proposed non-local framework, when coupled with
contact mechanics, can accurately capture hysteresis, energy dissipation, and recovery patterns in
cyclic loading scenarios, as well as recapitulate experimentally observed phase transitions as well
as metastable and bistable responses for architected materials. The contrast between metastable and
bistable materials highlights the role of energy landscape topology in governing reversibility. To
better approximate irreversible behaviors seen in real-world architected materials, we next examine
how artificial viscosity can be leveraged to mimic rate-effects at the microstructure level.

8.4.5. Approximating microstructural deformation rate effect

Even though the materials examined here are purely elastic, the extreme deformations that they
undergo at the microstructural level to accommodate densification and phase transformations,
can lead to rate-dependent dissipation associated with the non-affine microstructural deformation
mechanisms. To explore how artificial viscosity can emulate the aforementioned rate-effects in
architected materials, we perform comparative simulations for both metastable and bistable systems
under cyclic compression. Specifically, we vary the artificial viscosity parameter 𝜂 to assess its
influence on the loading–unloading response and energy dissipation characteristics.

Fig. 8-11 presents the force–displacement responses for two representative values of 𝜂, highlighting
distinct hysteresis behavior in both metastable Fig. 8-11(a) and bistable Fig. 8-11(b) systems. The
solid curves represent the loading path, while dashed lines denote the unloading phase. For lower
viscosity (𝜂 = 10.0), both material systems exhibit sharper softening transitions during loading
and faster recovery during unloading, resulting in narrower hysteresis loops. However, as viscosity
increases (𝜂 = 50.0 and 𝜂 = 120.0), the system begins to exhibit a more gradual force decay during
loading and significant residual deformation upon unloading—characteristics analogous to what
a viscous micro-deformation mechanism would induce. The stable segments of the path (elastic

 122

 Figure 8-10. Cyclic response for a bistable material system with 5.0% vertical grading in bulk
modulus 𝜅 and viscosity 𝜂 = 5.0. Loading and unloading are applied through a flat-top indenter in
  contact. The top plot shows the force–displacement hysteresis, and snapshots of 𝐽 reveal
 permanent deformation and residual fronts.

 123

Figure 8-11. Illustrating the approximation of a viscous microbuckling effect in (a) metastable and
  (b) bistable materials. Force–displacement curves are shown for increasing values of artificial
 viscosity 𝜂. Solid and dashed lines correspond to loading and unloading phases, respectively.

loading and densification), remain essentially unchanged by this modification of artificial viscosity
maintaining fidelity to the elastic solution.
The metastable case demonstrates a pronounced plateau and delayed re-stiffening in the high-
viscosity regime, while the bistable case features a more abrupt force drop followed by a long
plateau. Notably, the increased hysteresis area at higher 𝜂 reflects greater energy dissipation.
These observations suggest that artificial viscosity can be used not only as a regularization mech-
anism but also as a phenomenological means of emulating microstructural rate-effects. By tuning
𝜂, one can modulate hysteresis and residual strain—offering new pathways to model complex
metamaterials at the structural scale, and not limited to small RVEs.

8.4.6. Imperfection insensitive structures and auxetic effects

Metamaterials like foams and lattices show imperfection sensitivity [179]. Our proposed model
was shown to effectively capture such effects. On the other hand, there are architected materials
and metamaterials that have a more ordered/collective behavior. This is often the case when a well
designed mechanism for the microstructural deformation is at play governed by response-type and
not limit load-type behavior [35]. Such an effect was observed in Chapter 5 for soft metamaterials
exploiting buckling instabilities to undergo programmable pattern transformations. To investigate
the emergence of imperfection-insensitive mechanical response and auxetic behavior, we take
motivation for the use of gradient-enhanced damage models for fracture, and specifically towards
capturing imperfection sensitivity. In those case tuning the non-local length scale 𝑙 can effectively
filter out defects/imperfection with a characteristic length below that length scale[193].
We simulate the compression of a structured metastable system subjected to uniform vertical loading
through a frictionless flat punch. In this configuration, no geometric or material imperfections are
introduced, allowing us to isolate the intrinsic response of the architecture. A key distinction in
this case is the choice of the non-local length scale, which is set equal to the full height of the
domain 𝑙 = 𝐻. This large interaction length promotes filtering imperfections induced by numerical
error and penalizing large gradients of the non-local volume ratio. This effectively suppresses the
sensitivity to localized heterogeneities or numerical noise, inducing an order collective response
insensitive to imperfections.

 124

 Figure 8-12. Auxetic response in a metastable architected structure under uniform compression,
with its schematic illustrated in (a). Force–displacement curve, showcased in (b), is annotated with
  numbered markers corresponding to snapshots, presented in (c), showing the evolution of the
 Jacobian field 𝐽. The system uses a large non-local length scale equal to the domain height,
 promoting imperfection-insensitive and coordinated deformation.

 125

Fig. 8-12 illustrates the normalized force–displacement response alongside eight deformation snap-
shots that capture the evolution of the Jacobian determinant 𝐽. The structure exhibits a smooth,
progressive densification pattern without the emergence of a phase transformation front. The force
is increasing up to a point of an instability accommodating an auxetic effect. The Poisson’s effects
through this unconfined compression experiment are non-monotonic. Initially, lateral expansion is
observed during stable elastic loading, followed by a transition into an auxetic deformation mode
where lateral contraction is observed, induced by the instability and collective phase transformation
.

This study underscores the ability of long non-local interaction lengths to suppress imperfection-
driven instabilities and facilitate coordinated, auxetic deformation modes. The resulting behavior
is robust, and less sensitive to geometric or material noise, making it well-suited for adaptive
or morphing applications. These insights round out the comprehensive evaluation of the frame-
work, illustrating its versatility in capturing both localized and global deformation mechanisms in
architected materials.

8.5. Conclusion

In this study, we proposed and validated a non-local finite element framework tailored to simulate
the complex loading response of elastic architected materials and metamaterials. The framework
was specialized to capture metastable and bistable behaviors associated with volumetric phase
transformations between a dense and a rare phase. The formulation introduces a non-local volume
ratio and incorporates gradient-based regularization through penalization of the gradients of the
non-local volume ratio, enabling the resolution of sharp internal transitions such as nucleation of
dense phase and propagation of densified front under compressive loading. Through a comprehen-
sive series of numerical experiments, we demonstrated the robustness, accuracy, and adaptability
of the framework across a range of material parameters and mesh topologies. Artificial viscosity
was utilized as a tunable parameter to emulate rate-dependent micro-deformation phenomena, that
control dissipation. The model was shown to recapitulate phase transformation that emerge in
architected materials following the macroscopic response and appropriate signatures of the mi-
crostructural response, while being able to capture sharp phase transformation fronts and hysteresis
loops for metastable and bistable models.

By introducing heterogeneity of the materials’ properties, and more specifically, we further explored
imperfection sensitivity showcased how grading of material properties can guide densification. Fi-
nally, simulations with large a large non-local length scale relative to the domain size, demonstrated
the emergence of globally coordinated deformation and auxetic response. Notably, this framework
is the first to introduced a structured and scalable phenomenological construction to capture macro-
scopic and microscopic behavior for architected materials and metamaterials. Once an appropriate
energy is constructed and relevant non-local variables and length scales are identified, the model can
perform predictive calculation in a structural scale without having to resort to discrete calculations.
Additionally, it overcomes barriers encountered by local homogenization methods towards captur-
ing the emergence of localized phase transformation fronts opening the door for the development
of new theories and models.

 126

Overall, this work establishes a versatile computational foundation for capturing a broad spectrum of
mechanical phenomena in architected solids and metamaterials, particularly under loading regimes
where phase transitions, localization, and non-local interactions play a central role. These insights
lay the foundational work for future efforts in incorporating material heterogeneity, dynamic as well
as and inelastic effects into advanced continuum models for architected materials. Additionally,
incorporating machine learning approaches in the transitions from discrete RVE or experimental
data to the identification of non-local continuum models for accelerating and automating the model
development process is a task that we are planning to undertake in future studies.

 127

 128

9. CONCLUSION

9.1. Project Legacy

The overall conclusion of this report and legacy of the project is the following:

 Highly nonlinear phenomena can be incorporated into a gradient-based optimization
 approach to computational design if care is taken with respect to design parameteriza-
 tions, robust nonlinear solvers, and problem formulations.

This was repeatedly demonstrated throughout chapters 5, 6, and 7 for different optimization-based
design approaches that harness snap-through, snap-back, buckling, and self-contact.

9.2. Future Work

Continued investment in robust and differentiable physics simulations will enable the understanding
gained and techniques developed in this work to affect important mission spaces such as the design
of DIW compression pads. Moreover, such investment will be crucial for enabling future digital
workflows driven by AI/ML where simulations will play a crucial role.
The algorithms developed throughout this work were implemented in the OptimiSM code base
[249]. Development in such a research code enables rapid prototyping of new ideas, but limits
their applicability to relatively academic problems. Further investment is needed to translate
the successful algorithms and approaches developed throughout this work into Sandia production
codes.
Several interesting directions for future work focused on further increasing robustness can be
imagined. For instance, while the Trust Region solver employed throughout this work can robustly
handle large configurational changes and distinguish between stable and unstable equilibrium
configurations, there is currently no way of addressing element inversion beyond simple reduction
in the step size. An example of this issue could be observed in some optimization runs from
Chapter 7 with the square lattice cell. Extremizing the crush force efficiency could lead to material
distributions where localized regions of soft material led to highly distorted elements which caused
the solver to fail due to an inability to find a configuration where elements don’t invert (Figure
9-1, Figure 9-2). Techniques such as adaptive remeshing could help in this case, but whether such
remeshing would need to be done in a differentiable way or not is an open question. Additionally,
techniques from the computer graphics community such as an invertible extension of elastic strain
energy [241] or an inversion-aware extension of the Trust Region algorithm based on the line
search approaches in [236, 210, 211] could also help. Additionally, adopting methods to ensure

Figure A.0.1. Uniaxial stretch of a material point: Comparison of analytic and numerical solutions
 for 11-component of inelastic logarithmic strain.

 155

To verify that the constitutive model was implemented correctly, Figure A.0.1 compares the inelastic
logarithmic strain computed numerically to that obtained by Eq. A.13 above. Here, a Weissenberg
number 𝑊𝑖 = 1 × 10−2 is considered and 100 time steps are used in the numerical evaluation. It can
be seen that the numerical implementation matches closely the analytic solution in Eq. A.13.

 (a) (b)

Figure A.0.2. Uniaxial stretch of a material point: Comparison of analytic and numerical solutions
 for (a) 11-component of elastic logarithmic strain, (b) dissipation.

Having an analytic solution also allows us to investigate the convergence of the exponential map
integration scheme. This is done by comparing two quantities - the logarithmic elastic strain and
the dissipation - to the analytic values for a different number of time steps. Figure A.0.2 shows
the results, where it can be seen that the elastic logarithmic strain matches the analytic solution
with a small number of time steps. The dissipation, however, requires significantly more time
steps to converge to the analytic solution. This is due to the fact that time steps smaller than the
relaxation time are needed to resolve the dissipation numerically. For lower Weissenberg numbers
corresponding to longer loading times, a large number of time steps may be needed to resolve the
dissipation. However, the state computed by the constitutive algorithm can be converged with a
relatively small number of time steps, as evidenced by the elastic logarithmic strain.

 156

APPENDIX B. Sensitivity Verification

B.1. Verification for CAD-Parameterized Shape Optimization

Finite difference tests are utilized to confirm correct implementation of sensitivities. This is done
by choosing a random direction along which to perturb the design parameters and computing the
Newton quotient for a range of finite difference step sizes. The Newton quotient is computed as

                                     𝑓 (𝝓 + ℎ𝒅) − 𝑓 (𝝓) 𝑑𝑓
 − ·𝒅 (B.1)
 ℎ 𝑑𝝓

Finite difference tests are conducted on a domain consisting of a single unit cell from the 5x5 array
of pores subject to uniaxial compression with 15 % strain and Weissenberg number 𝑊𝑖 = 1 × 10−2 .
First, correct implementation of the adjoint sensitivity analysis presented in Section 4.4 to compute
𝑑𝑓 /𝑑 𝑿 is verified through finite difference tests. Three different objective functions 𝑓 are checked:
the L2 difference norm under monotonic loading, the total work under cyclic loading, and the
dissipation efficiency under cyclic loading (see Sections 4.3 and 5.3.2). Figure B.1.1a shows the
results, which display the expected behavior of a finite difference approximation when compared
to an analytic gradient: a decrease in error as the step size is reduced and a resulting increase once
the step size is small enough to be in round-off error.

 (a) (b)

 Figure B.1.1. Finite difference test results for three different objective functions: (a) check of
 𝑑𝑓 /𝑑 𝑿, (b) check of 𝑑𝑓 /𝑑𝝓

Next, finite difference tests are used to verify correct implementation of the full sensitivities 𝑑𝑓 /𝑑𝝓
(see Figure 4-1) used by the optimizer. This takes into account the gradient 𝑑 𝑿/𝑑𝝓 provided by
ESP as well as the application of the chain rule when computing 𝑑𝑓 /𝑑𝝓. For these tests, a single

 157

design parameter 𝝓 corresponding to the superellipse radius is used and perturbed to compute finite
difference values. Figure B.1.1b shows the results for all three objectives, which again display the
expected behavior of a finite difference approximation when compared to an analytic gradient. It
should be noted that these finite difference tests were conducted by morphing the existing mesh to
the perturbed geometry rather than generating a new mesh. That is, the same mesh topology is
used upon perturbation, but the nodes in this mesh are mapped to new locations to accommodate
the perturbed geometry. This is in contrast to the remeshing approach used during optimization,
which introduces inconsistencies between the discretization error present when computing 𝑑𝑓 /𝑑𝝓
and 𝑓 (𝝓), and that present when computing 𝑓 (𝝓 + ℎ𝒅). Such an inconsistency manifests in a
loss of precision in gradient computations, and can only be mitigated through mesh refinement.
However, for the purposes of optimization-based design, gradients are mostly used to seek out new
regions of the design space by finding local minima. Once near a local minima it is less important
to reduce the objective value with as much precision as possible. In this light, some discretization
error can be tolerated. For applications requiring more precision in gradients in order to reduce
objective function values as much as possible, an approach that switches to mesh morphing once
near enough to a local minimum can be employed.
